JCPThree strategies for claiming research progress
2026-06-18
David Gal, Bobby Calder, Brian Sternthal
AI
EN

This paper addresses how consumer researchers can justify claims of research progress by identifying three distinct strategies: establishing-an-effect (demonstrating replicable X-Y relationships), theory-of-the-effect (specifying local mechanistic accounts), and theoretical framework (developing explanations transcending single effects). Through coding recent articles in leading consumer research journals, the authors find that the field overwhelmingly relies on the theory-of-the-effect strategy while rarely advancing theoretical frameworks. The paper clarifies the core logic, methodological emphases, and vulnerabilities of each strategy, and provides actionable guidance for progressing from theories of effects to broader frameworks. The contribution lies in arguing that deliberately combining these strategies provides a stronger foundation for claiming meaningful research progress.

JA

本論文は、消費者研究者が研究の進歩をどのような根拠で主張できるかという問いに対し、3つの戦略を提示している。具体的には、効果の確立（変数XとYの関係の再現性）、効果の理論（局所的なメカニズムの特定）、理論的フレームワーク（単一の効果を超えた説明）である。主要な消費者研究ジャーナルの最近の論文をコーディング分析した結果、当該分野は効果の理論戦略に圧倒的に依存しており、理論的フレームワークの構築はほとんど行われていないことが明らかになった。各戦略の論理・方法論的重点・脆弱性を明確化し、効果の理論からフレームワークへ発展させるための実践的指針を提供することで、3つの戦略を意図的に組み合わせることが研究進歩の主張をより強固にすると論じている。

JCPGender identity, overconfidence, and risk attitudes
2026-06-16
Edward R. Lawrence, Benedikt Wick
AI
EN

This study investigates the relationship between gender identity, overconfidence, and investment risk-taking behaviors using a measure of overconfidence defined as the gap between perceived and actual financial knowledge. The empirical analysis reveals that nonbinary individuals exhibit significantly lower levels of financial overconfidence compared to those identifying as male or female, attributable to more modest self-assessment rather than differences in actual knowledge. The findings show that higher overconfidence correlates with greater willingness to take financial risks, and nonbinary individuals demonstrate considerably lower risk-taking inclination in investment decisions. This research contributes to the literature by extending the analysis of gender effects in financial decision-making beyond the traditional binary framework and ruling out mental health challenges and generic minority effects as explanations.

JA

本研究は、ジェンダー・アイデンティティと過信、投資リスク行動の関係を、認識された金融知識と実際の金融知識の乖離を過信の指標として用いて検証した。実証分析の結果、ノンバイナリーの個人は男性・女性と自認する人々と比較して金融に関する過信が有意に低く、これは実際の知識の差ではなく、より控えめな自己評価に起因することが明らかになった。過信が高いほど金融リスクを取る意欲が高まり、ノンバイナリーの個人は投資判断においてリスクを取る傾向が著しく低いことが示された。本研究は、金融意思決定におけるジェンダー効果の分析を従来の二項対立的枠組みを超えて拡張し、メンタルヘルスの課題やマイノリティ効果では説明できないことを示した点で貢献している。

JMREXPRESS: Consumer Interactions and Peer Effects in Socially-Connected Digital Products
2026-06-16
Yijun Chen, Yulia Nevskaya
AI
EN

This paper investigates peer effects in user churn on a socially-connected digital platform and examines how firms can leverage network structures for customer relationship management. Using data from a massively-multiplayer online game, the authors develop a two-stage structural model that captures endogenous network formation and peer effects in churn, correcting for endogeneity bias through unobserved gamer heterogeneity. Key findings reveal that peer effects substantially amplify intervention effectiveness with an average social multiplier of 2.48, and that users vary more in their influence on peers' retention than in their own retention likelihood. The study contributes to CRM literature by demonstrating that a user's network value is primarily driven by network centrality measures rather than individual characteristics, offering actionable insights for targeted retention strategies.

JA

本論文は、ソーシャル機能を持つデジタルプラットフォームにおけるユーザー離脱のピア効果を調査し、企業がネットワーク構造を顧客関係管理（CRM）に活用する方法を検討している。大規模マルチプレイヤーオンラインゲームのデータを用い、内生的なネットワーク形成とピア効果を捉える2段階構造モデルを構築し、観測されないゲーマーの異質性を通じて内生性バイアスを補正した。主要な発見として、ピア効果が介入効果を大幅に増幅させ（平均ソーシャル乗数2.48）、ユーザーは自身の継続可能性よりも他者の継続に与える影響において大きな差異があることが示された。本研究は、ユーザーのネットワーク価値が個人特性よりもネットワーク中心性指標によって主に決定されることを明らかにし、CRM分野に新たな知見を提供している。

JMEXPRESS: People Like Us: Heightened Preference for Ingroup Providers in Mental Healthcare
2026-06-16
Phyllis Xue Wang, Zhengyu Shi, Jinjie Chen, Qiyuan Wang
AI
EN

This research investigates how consumers' preferences for ingroup versus outgroup healthcare providers differ between mental and physical healthcare seeking. Using approximately 25 million medical consultation records and 11 randomized controlled experiments, the study found that consumers place greater importance on provider empathy when seeking mental healthcare, leading to stronger preferences for ingroup providers. This effect was attenuated when provider empathy ratings were displayed or among consumers with higher global identity. The research contributes to understanding mental healthcare decision-making and offers practical implications for reducing health disparities.

JA

本研究は、精神的疾患と身体的疾患の医療を求める際に、消費者の内集団と外集団の医療提供者に対する選好がどのように異なるかを検証した。約2500万件の医療相談データと11の無作為化比較実験を用いた結果、消費者は精神的疾患の医療を求める際に医療提供者の共感性をより重視し、内集団の提供者を強く選好することが明らかになった。この効果は、提供者の共感性評価が提示された場合や、グローバル・アイデンティティが高い消費者において減弱した。本研究はメンタルヘルスケアの意思決定に関する理解を深め、健康格差の軽減に向けた実践的示唆を提供している。

JCRDefining and Understanding Vintage
2026-06-15
Maren Hoff, Silvia Bellezza
AI
EN

This research addresses the question of what defines vintage products and how they differ from related concepts such as antique, secondhand, and retro items. Using a multimethod approach including interviews, surveys, experiments, and analysis of over 2,000 vintage products across 16 categories, the authors develop a unified framework identifying vintage as a composite formative construct determined by three dimensions: moderate age, iconicity, and scarcity. The key findings reveal that vintage products deliver personal benefits (heritage connection, authenticity), social benefits (coolness), and sustainability benefits (environmental responsibility), and that emphasizing these three dimensions enhances consumer responses more effectively for vintage than for new products. This research contributes a theoretical foundation for understanding vintage consumption and provides practical guidance for identifying and positioning vintage products in the marketplace.

JA

本研究は、ヴィンテージ製品とは何か、またアンティーク、中古品、レトロといった関連概念とどのように異なるのかという問いに取り組んでいる。インタビュー、調査、実験、16カテゴリにわたる2,000点以上のヴィンテージ製品の分析を含むマルチメソッドアプローチを用いて、ヴィンテージを「適度な古さ」「象徴性（アイコニシティ）」「希少性」の3次元によって規定される複合的形成構成概念として定義する統一的フレームワークを開発した。主要な発見として、ヴィンテージ製品は個人的便益（遺産とのつながり、本物らしさ）、社会的便益（クールさ）、持続可能性の便益（環境責任）をもたらし、これら3次元を強調することで新製品よりも消費者反応が高まることが示された。本研究はヴィンテージ消費を理解するための理論的基盤を提供し、市場におけるヴィンテージ製品の識別とポジショニングに実践的な指針を与える貢献をしている。

P&MDo Not Think Ahead of Me: High‐Personalization Chatbots Can Backfire During the Goal Formation Stage
2026-06-15
Xingyu Chen, Minxue Huang, Xiaodong Xie
AIartificial intelligence
EN

This research investigates whether high-personalization AI chatbots are always beneficial for consumers across different decision stages. Through five studies, the authors find that high-personalization chatbots can backfire during the goal formation stage but not during the goal fulfillment stage, with reduced decision autonomy serving as the underlying mechanism. The study also reveals that consumers with interdependent selves (vs. independent selves) moderate this negative effect. These findings contribute to the chatbot personalization literature by highlighting the importance of considering specific decision stages when deploying AI chatbots.

JA

本研究は、高度にパーソナライズされたAIチャットボットが消費者の意思決定段階全体において常に有益であるかを検証した。5つの実験を通じて、高パーソナライゼーションのチャットボットは目標達成段階では問題ないが、目標形成段階では逆効果になることを発見し、その原因が意思決定の自律性の低下にあることを明らかにした。また、相互依存的自己（vs. 独立的自己）を持つ消費者がこの負の効果を緩和することも示された。本研究は、AIチャットボット導入において特定の意思決定段階を考慮する重要性を示し、チャットボットのパーソナライゼーション研究に貢献している。

P&MAging and Sustainable Consumption: An Investigation of the Role of Time Perspective
2026-06-15
Alessandro M. Peluso, Yeonjin Sung, Cesare Amatulli, Gianluigi Guido, Carolyn Yoon
AI
EN

This research examines how time perspective affects sustainable consumption among older consumers. Through five studies, the authors demonstrate that older consumers engage less in sustainable consumption due to adopting a shorter time perspective, but message cues prompting a longer time perspective can increase their environmentally friendly choices. The studies also reveal that perceived youthfulness moderates this relationship, with older consumers who feel younger being more likely to engage in sustainable consumption. These findings contribute theoretical insights to the aging and sustainability literature while offering practical implications for marketers targeting the growing older consumer segment.

JA

本研究は、時間的展望が高齢消費者のサステナブル消費にどのような影響を与えるかを検討している。5つの研究を通じて、高齢消費者は短い時間的展望を採用するためサステナブル消費に従事しにくいが、長期的な時間的展望を促すメッセージが環境配慮型選択を増加させることを実証した。また、主観的若さ（実年齢より若く感じる傾向）がこの関係を調整し、自分を若いと感じる高齢者ほどサステナブル消費に従事しやすいことを明らかにした。これらの知見は、高齢化と持続可能性に関する理論的貢献を提供するとともに、増加する高齢消費者層へのマーケティング実務に示唆を与える。

JCPThe continuity heuristic: Temporal extrapolation in consumer judgment
2026-06-14
Matthew Fisher, Adam H. Smiley
AI
EN

This research investigates how consumers predict the future longevity of products, brands, and services. Across 24 studies with 9,718 participants, the authors demonstrate the 'continuity heuristic'—a mental shortcut where consumers use past duration as a basis for forecasting future continuation. The effect persists across diverse domains including brand heritage, product obsolescence, subscriptions, and trends, even when quality is controlled and age is not explicitly mentioned. The authors propose a question-substitution mechanism and show the effect weakens for technology products (with clear lifespan norms) and expert consumers, contributing to temporal judgment psychology and offering marketers insights for predicting consumer preferences and perceived durability.

JA

本研究は、消費者が製品・ブランド・サービスの将来的な持続期間をどのように予測するかを検討した。24の研究（N=9,718）を通じて、消費者が過去の存続期間を基に将来の継続を予測する「継続性ヒューリスティック」を実証した。この効果は、ブランドの伝統、製品の陳腐化、サブスクリプション継続、トレンド予測など多様な領域で確認され、品質を統制し年数を明示しない場合でも持続した。著者らは質問置換メカニズムを提唱し、明確な寿命規範がある技術製品や専門知識を持つ消費者では効果が弱まることを示し、時間的判断の心理学およびマーケティング実務への貢献を果たした。

P&MThe Impact of Artificial Intelligence Co‐Experience on Consumer Loneliness: The Role of Culture and Experience Valence
2026-06-10
Yiming Qian, Carlos Velasco, Xiaoang Wan
AIartificial intelligence
EN

This research investigates whether AI-mediated co-experience can reduce consumer loneliness and improve service evaluations, and how cultural contexts moderate these effects. Through three studies employing both national-level cultural comparisons and individual-level cultural value measurements, the authors examined consumer responses to AI co-experience in positive and negative service contexts. Key findings reveal that AI co-experience enhances service satisfaction, with collectivist consumers experiencing lower loneliness and stronger continuance intention than individualist consumers, and that external attribution of responsibility leads to more favorable responses. The research contributes by extending the co-experience framework to AI services and advancing cultural theory as a moderator in AI-consumer interactions.

JA

本研究は、AIを介した共同体験が消費者の孤独感を軽減しサービス評価を向上させるか、またその効果が文化的文脈によってどう異なるかを検討した。国家レベルの文化比較と個人レベルの文化的価値観測定を用いた3つの研究を通じて、ポジティブおよびネガティブなサービス状況におけるAI共同体験への消費者反応を分析した。主要な発見として、AI共同体験はサービス満足度を高め、集団主義的消費者は個人主義的消費者より低い孤独感と強い継続利用意図を示すこと、また責任の外的帰属がより好意的な反応につながることが明らかになった。本研究は共同体験の枠組みをAIサービス領域に拡張し、文化をモデレーターとして理論化した点で貢献している。

JMEXPRESS: The Marketization of Tradition: How Firms Reconfigure Tradition to Create Consumer Value
2026-06-05
Michelle I.C. Yang, Julien Cayla
AI
EN

This study examines how marketers can create consumer value from tradition without sliding into crass commodification. Using ethnographic research on zuò yuèzi (Chinese postpartum confinement) across Singapore, Taipei, and Kuala Lumpur, the authors investigate the booming confinement center industry that has emerged to address tensions between traditional practices and contemporary ideals of autonomy, health, and scientific expertise. Drawing on practice theory, the key finding reveals that successful commercial interventions depend on adapting key elements of tradition while preserving its teleoaffective structure—the moral purposes and affective orientations that define what a practice is for and how it should feel. The study contributes a more constructive theorization of marketization that highlights possibilities for value creation in the tradition domain.

JA

本研究は、マーケターがいかにして伝統から消費者価値を創造しつつ、過度な商品化を回避できるかを検討している。シンガポール、台北、クアラルンプールにおいて、中国の産後養生習慣「坐月子（zuò yuèzi）」と急成長する産後ケアセンター産業についてエスノグラフィー調査を実施した。実践理論を援用し、商業的介入の有効性は伝統の主要要素を適応させつつも、その「目的感情構造（teleoaffective structure）」—すなわち伝統的実践の道徳的目的と感情的志向性—を維持することに依存することを明らかにした。本研究の貢献は、伝統領域における価値創造の可能性を強調する、より建設的な市場化の理論化を提示した点にある。

JMEXPRESS: Surviving Economic Adversity: Governance of Franchise Clusters
2026-06-05
Xu (Vivian) Zheng, Yajing Fan, Mrinal Ghosh
AI
EN

This study examines how franchise governance mechanisms affect outlet survival during economic downturns. Using a novel clustering algorithm and survival analysis on data from 8,677 outlets across 18 franchisors over 14 years, the authors find that franchisee ownership fragmentation reduces outlet failure hazard in dense clusters during economic adversity, while franchisor on-site supervision increases failure hazard in such contexts. The research contributes to franchise governance literature by identifying cluster-level mechanisms that moderate the relationship between economic conditions and outlet survival, and offers practical tools including a cluster identification app called FranClusterer.

JA

本研究は、経済的逆境時においてフランチャイズのガバナンスメカニズムが店舗存続にどのような影響を与えるかを検証している。18のフランチャイザーにおける8,677店舗の14年間のデータを用い、新規クラスタリングアルゴリズムと生存分析を適用した結果、経済状況悪化時には、フランチャイジーの所有分散化が密集クラスター内の店舗閉鎖リスクを低減する一方、フランチャイザーの現地監督強化はむしろ閉鎖リスクを高めることが明らかになった。本研究は、経済環境と店舗存続の関係を調整するクラスターレベルのメカニズムを特定し、FranClustererという実用的なクラスター識別ツールを提供することで、フランチャイズガバナンス研究に貢献している。

JMEXPRESS: Personalization in Marketing Communication: A Meta-Analysis
2026-06-05
Martin Eisend, Dominika Niewiadomska, Guda van Noort
AI
EN

This meta-analysis examines the personalization paradox in marketing communication, where personalization may enhance persuasion through relevance but potentially trigger privacy concerns. Integrating 1,536 effect sizes from 290 studies across 229 papers, the research finds that personalization generally leads to persuasion success with negligible negative effects. Key findings reveal that personalization is most effective when data is tied to consumers' self-concept, and persuasion follows a J-shaped pattern with strongest effects at high personalization levels. This study provides empirical generalizations that resolve theoretical ambiguity and offer practical guidance for marketers on when personalization is most effective.

JA

本メタ分析は、マーケティングコミュニケーションにおけるパーソナライゼーションが関連性を高め説得を促進する一方でプライバシー懸念を引き起こす可能性があるという「パーソナライゼーション・パラドックス」を検証した。229本の論文から抽出された290研究・1,536の効果量を統合した結果、パーソナライゼーションは全体として説得成功につながり、負の効果は無視できる程度であることが明らかになった。また、消費者の自己概念に密接に関連するデータを用いた場合に効果が高まること、および説得効果はJ字型パターンを示し高度なパーソナライゼーションで最も効果的であることが判明した。本研究は理論的曖昧さを解消する実証的一般化を提供し、マーケターにパーソナライゼーション活用の実践的指針を示す貢献をしている。

P&MServant, Partner, or Competitor? How Embodied AI Role Positioning Influences Consumer's Purchase Intention Through Threat Perception and Feelings of Dominance
2026-06-05
Crystal T. Lee, Yung‐Cheng Shen
AIartificial intelligence
EN

This research investigates how different social role positioning of embodied AI (servant, partner, or competitor) influences consumer purchase intentions. Through four experimental studies grounded in social dominance theory, the authors demonstrate that servant AI generates the most favorable consumer responses via enhanced dominance perception and reduced threat, while competitor AI produces the least favorable outcomes. The studies further reveal that these effects are moderated by visual design morphology, humor modality, and individual mastery orientation, with curved designs and affiliative humor mitigating threat responses for competitor AI. This work contributes by establishing dominance signaling as a critical theoretical framework for understanding consumer-AI relationships and offering practical guidance for AI product positioning strategies.

JA

本研究は、具現化されたAI（embodied AI）の社会的役割ポジショニング（従者・パートナー・競争相手）が消費者の購買意図にどのように影響するかを検討した。社会的支配理論に基づく4つの実験研究を通じて、従者AIが支配感の向上と脅威認知の低減を介して最も好意的な消費者反応を生み出し、競争相手AIが最も低い評価を得ることを明らかにした。さらに、これらの効果は視覚デザインの形態（角張った形状vs.曲線的形状）、ユーモアの様式（攻撃的vs.親和的）、および個人の熟達志向性によって調整されることが示された。本研究は、支配シグナリングを消費者-AI関係理解の重要な理論的枠組みとして確立し、AI製品のポジショニング戦略に実践的示唆を提供する点で貢献している。

P&MToo Caring to Be Real? Autonomy‐Supportive AI, Intimacy Gains, and Authenticity Loss
2026-06-05
Thanh Tiep Le, Duy Khánh Phan, Bao Quoc Truong-Dinh
AIartificial intelligence
EN

This research investigates why autonomy-supportive and empathic AI communication simultaneously enhances perceived intimacy yet undermines perceived authenticity beyond a certain threshold. Using a staggered field rollout, longitudinal panel data, and a between-subjects experiment (N=207), the authors employ event-study Difference-in-Differences, System GMM, and experimental manipulation to estimate causal effects of Perceived Autonomy Support in AI Communication (PAS-AI). Results demonstrate that moderate PAS-AI increases relational intimacy through greater conversational elaboration and re-engagement, but overly consistent or fluent autonomy-supportive cues trigger authenticity decline, with users perceiving responses as scripted or 'too caring to be real.' The study contributes by reframing empathic AI as a matter of relational calibration rather than imitation, and shows that human-in-the-loop escalation and transparent AI disclosure can preserve relational trust.

JA

本研究は、自律性支援的で共感的なAIコミュニケーションが知覚された親密性を高める一方で、一定の閾値を超えると知覚された真正性を損なう理由を検討している。段階的フィールド展開、縦断的パネルデータ、被験者間実験（N=207）を用い、イベントスタディ差分の差分法、システムGMM、実験的操作によりAIコミュニケーションにおける知覚された自律性支援（PAS-AI）の因果効果を推定した。結果として、適度なPAS-AIは会話の精緻化や自発的再エンゲージメントを通じて関係的親密性を高めるが、自律性支援的手がかりが過度に一貫性を持つか流暢になると、ユーザーは応答を台本的または「本物とは思えないほど気遣いがある」と感じ、真正性の低下が生じることが示された。本研究は共感的AIを模倣ではなく関係的調整の問題として再構成し、ヒューマン・イン・ザ・ループによるエスカレーションや透明なAI開示が関係的信頼を維持できることを示した点で貢献している。

P&MBeyond Black Boxes: AI Certification Labels Increase Adoption through Trust
2026-06-05
Filipa de Almeida, Joana Glaum, Kiall Hildred, Ian J. Scott
AIartificial intelligence
EN

This study investigates whether Trustworthy AI certification labels can increase end-users' trust in and intention to use AI systems. Through three experimental studies, the researchers found that simple certification labels significantly increase both cognitive and affective trust in AI, with cognitive trust mediating the effect on behavioral intention to use. Additionally, labels displaying specific Trustworthy AI principles further enhanced perceived trustworthiness, while exposure to labeled systems reduced trust in non-labeled alternatives. This research contributes to the literature on AI trust and acceptance by demonstrating certification labels as an effective non-technical solution for communicating AI trustworthiness to general users.

JA

JMREXPRESS: Modeling Dynamic Consumer Preferences from Few-shot Data: A Meta-Learning Approach
2026-06-03
Mingzhang Yin, Khaled Boughanmi, Anirban Mukherjee
AI
EN

This paper addresses the challenge of capturing and adapting to customer preferences when individual-level interaction data is limited, such as with new customers or short sessions. The authors propose Meta-Temporal Processes (MetaTP), a meta-learning framework that combines Transformer-based architecture to model sequential consumption patterns and enables personalization from few observations by transferring learned structure across customers. Empirical results on digital product consumption demonstrate that MetaTP outperforms benchmark methods in few-shot prediction tasks and reveals interpretable patterns of preference evolution. The contribution lies in providing firms with a scalable approach to optimize personalized recommendations during the 'lukewarm' customer journey stage, including product sequencing and session completion strategies.

JMEXPRESS: Power Distance Belief Affects Coupon Use
2026-06-03
Minkyung Koo, Ashok K. Lalwani, Sijie Sun
AI
EN

This research investigates how power distance belief (PDB) influences consumer coupon usage behavior. Using eight studies including field studies, scanner panel data, and experiments, the authors find that consumers with higher power distance belief are less likely to use coupons because they exhibit greater skepticism toward businesses' motives. The study also identifies boundary conditions and offers practical strategies for marketers, such as targeting low-PDB segments, activating low PDB through situational cues, reducing skepticism, and offering price-matching guarantees. This research contributes to both coupon marketing literature and cross-cultural consumer behavior by revealing a novel psychological mechanism underlying coupon redemption rates.

P&MHow Artificial Intelligence Influences Consumer Reflection in Decision‐Making: The Role of AI Anthropomorphism and Reinforcement
2026-06-02
En Chen, Jie Meng, Safak Dogan
AIartificial intelligence
EN

This research examines how AI anthropomorphism and reinforcement influence consumer reflexivity in decision-making. Study 1 validated a self-reflexivity model showing that reflections on historical, social, and ideal selves sequentially drive self-evaluation, emotional responses, and behavioral intentions. Study 2, using a 2×2 experiment, found that while the core reflexivity pathway remains structurally stable across conditions, measurement invariance testing revealed a meaning-shift in the ideal-self construct rather than simple amplification effects. The findings contribute to consumer identity research by distinguishing stable self-foundations from context-sensitive aspirational representations and offer guidance for designing human-centered AI recommender systems.

P&MFrom Real to Artificial: How Profile Image Realism Shapes Warmth, Competence, and Trade Outcomes in Swapping Platforms
2026-05-30
Sarah Alhouti, Louvins Pierre, X.Z. Liu
AI
EN

This research examines how profile image realism and value transparency jointly influence consumer perceptions and trade behavior on C2C swapping platforms. Using three studies combining behavioral data analysis and experiments, the authors find that realistic profile images enhance perceived warmth and competence, thereby increasing trade intentions, particularly when value transparency is low. P&MThe Role of Intrinsic Quality in Reducing Consumer Xenocentrism: Evidence From a New World Wine Market
2026-05-28
Alvaro Luis Lamas Cassago, Mateus Manfrin Artêncio, Richard Paul Bagozzi, Janaína de Moura Engracia Giraldi, Fernando B. Da Costa
AI
EN

This study examined whether presenting intrinsic quality information about New World wines as superior to Old World wines can reduce consumer xenocentrism (C-XEN) and increase domestic wine purchase intentions. Using a representative consumer sample in an experimental setting, the researchers compared foreign versus domestic wines through expert and science-based quality information, with moderators including product involvement, trust in science, and material self. The findings revealed that superior quality information successfully reduced C-XEN, but this reduction did not translate into significantly higher purchase intentions for domestic wines, suggesting that self-enhancement and social-aggrandizement motives associated with foreign products remain resilient. The study advances C-XEN theory by demonstrating the potential and limitations of scientific quality communication in reducing preference bias, offering practical implications for domestic product marketing strategies.

P&MMore Than Saying “It's AI”: How Role Disclosure Transparency in AI‐Generated Ads Influences Persuasion
2026-05-28

AI
EN

This research investigates how AI role disclosure transparency—consumers' perception of how clearly an ad communicates AI's role in its creation—affects persuasion in AI-generated advertisements. Through four studies, the authors demonstrate that higher perceived transparency enhances ad creation process credibility, which leads to more favorable attitudes toward the ad. The positive effects of transparency are amplified when the disclosure motive is framed as reactive (rather than proactive) and when regulatory compliance signals are present. This research contributes to the understanding of strategic disclosure in AI-assisted advertising and provides a decision-making framework for marketers to optimize consumer responses to AI-generated content.

2026-05-26

AI

This study investigates whether fMRI data can improve predictions of store manager adoption decisions and consumer sales of consumer packaged goods beyond traditional market and survey data. 2026-05-25
R Kevin Smith, Katherine Du
AI
EN

This paper addresses the question of how consumers form authenticity judgments, rather than focusing on what authenticity is. Through a systematic review of authenticity research integrated with dual-process theories, the authors propose a conceptual framework positioning authenticity judgments as proceeding through two routes: a fact-based route involving deliberate analytical evaluation and a feeling-based route relying on intuitive processing and emotional resonance. P&MHuman in the Loop, or Perceived Oversight? The Psychological Inference That Drives AI Credibility
2026-05-25
Guneet Kaur Nagpal, June Cotte
AI
EN

This research investigates the psychological mechanism through which human-in-the-loop (HITL) AI systems build credibility, challenging the assumption that human oversight inherently increases trust. Through two experiments using AI-generated investment research, the authors found that human-supervised AI actually produced the lowest credibility among all conditions, and that 'perceived oversight adequacy'—the inference that competent review occurred—is the dominant mediator of the source-to-credibility relationship. The key contribution is reframing the HITL debate from whether to add human oversight to how to effectively signal that the analytical process was sound.

P&MThe Silent Guardian: Symmetry's Subtle Influence on Safety Cues and Consumer Behavior
2026-05-23
Yi Huang, Hanwen Bi, Wei Zhao, Fenghua Wang, Yu Pan
AI
EN

This study investigates how visual symmetry in product presentations and brand imagery influences consumer behavior through perceived safety. Using a mixed-methods approach combining Google Trends analysis, archival data, and five laboratory experiments, the researchers demonstrate that symmetrical elements increase perceived safety, which in turn enhances consumer responses across various product categories. JCPRepentance‐driven fitness: Moral striving in a Tunisian context
2026-05-22
Hela Zouaoui, Russell Belk, Vitor Lima
AI
EN

This study examines how Islamic moral-spiritual meaning systems shape fitness consumption motivations and sustain ongoing moral striving among Muslim gymgoers. Using 13 months of ethnography and 17 interviews in a Tunisian gym, the researchers found that participants interpret exercise through Tawba (Islamic repentance and return), where diet lapses, missed sessions, and bodily discomfort become part of a recurring cycle of renewed intention and moral self-correction. P&MThe Embodiment of Vulnerability: How Consumers Judge Empathy Gaps in Artificial Intelligence‐Driven Healthcare
2026-05-20
Mariana Girão Carrilho, Diego Costa Pinto, Saleh Shuqair, Márcia Maurer Herter, Anna S. Mattila
AIartificial intelligence
EN

This research investigates how consumers perceive empathy from AI versus human healthcare providers depending on the type of vulnerability experienced. Drawing on Embodiment Theory and Mind-Body Dualism, the authors conducted two controlled experiments and text mining of health app reviews to test their framework. The key finding reveals a vulnerability-contingent dilemma: humans are perceived as more empathetic for embodied vulnerability (physical pain), while AI achieves comparable empathy perceptions for disembodied vulnerability (mental health contexts). This contributes to the literature by introducing the concept of 'embodiment of vulnerability' and demonstrating how it systematically shapes consumer judgments of empathic care capacity in AI-driven healthcare.

JA

P&MHow Divisible Donation Amounts Increase Charitable Giving
2026-05-20
Xiaohong Zhao, Zhiyong Yang, Shige Zhao
AI
EN

This research investigates how the divisibility of suggested donation amounts affects charitable giving behavior. Through seven studies combining field and experimental methods, the authors demonstrate that divisible amounts (e.g., $20) increase donations compared to non-divisible amounts (e.g., $19), mediated by donors' perceived breadth of impact and fairness. The effect is moderated by need type, being attenuated for survival needs but amplified for developmental needs. These findings contribute to the charitable giving literature by identifying a novel numerical factor influencing donation decisions and offer practical guidance for nonprofit organizations in designing effective fundraising appeals.

P&MWhen Social Media Ideals Backfire: How Idealized Digital Standards Undermine Self‐Efficacy and Goal Attainability
2026-05-19
Rafael Wagner, Diego Costa Pinto, Saleh Shuqair, Ana Valenzuela, Barry J. Babin
AI
EN

This research examines how exposure to idealized standards on social media affects consumers' motivation during long-term goal pursuit. Using five studies including text-mining analysis and controlled experiments, the authors find that idealized digital representations undermine motivation by inflating perceived goal distance and eroding self-efficacy, rather than by increasing perceived effort. JMREXPRESS: Where Should Firms Implement Differential Privacy in Targeting? Implications for Profitability
2026-05-18
Gilian R. Ponte, Tom Boot, Thomas Reutterer, Jaap E. Wieringa
AI
EN

This study investigates where firms should implement differential privacy within their targeting workflow to balance privacy protection and profitability. Using a large-scale field experiment with 747,975 customers and simulations, the authors compare two novel targeting strategies: implementing differential privacy during model training versus at the targeting-decision stage. The key finding reveals that the decision-stage implementation yields approximately fivefold higher profits than the training-stage approach while maintaining strong privacy protection. The research contributes by deriving expected profit formulas for given privacy risk levels and introducing a privacy elasticity measure, providing practical guidelines for firms and policymakers on privacy-preserving targeting.

JMEXPRESS: First-Party Content Production in a Competitive Media Market
2026-05-18
Xuelian Qin, Lin Tian, Bo Zhou
AI
EN

This paper investigates the optimal first-party (original) content production strategy for asymmetric competing video streaming distributors using an analytical model. The study reveals that existing content overlap and market price rigidity are critical factors affecting optimal strategies: high price rigidity combined with content overlap strengthens incentives for original content production, while low price rigidity with flexible pricing weakens these incentives. Importantly, when prices are optimally adjusted, first-party and third-party content shift from substitutes to complements, potentially creating a win-win-win outcome for distributors, third-party producers, and consumers. These findings challenge conventional assumptions about content competition and offer a strategic framework for managers to optimize content production investments based on resource asymmetry and market pricing dynamics.

JMEXPRESS: Product Portfolio Choices with Product Life Cycles
2026-05-18
Peichun Wang
AI
EN

This study examines how multi-product firms adjust their product portfolios in response to increased competition, focusing on the role of product life cycles (PLCs). Using data from the Chinese smartphone market, the author develops a structural model that incorporates firms' PLC expectations into product portfolio competition decisions. Key findings reveal that ignoring portfolio adjustments overstates consumer welfare gains from competition by 70%, and holding PLC expectations fixed understates firms' portfolio responses by 10% while overstating welfare gains by 9%. The research contributes to policy evaluation by demonstrating that increased low-end competition leads incumbent firms to introduce lower-quality 'fighting brands,' highlighting the importance of considering dynamic product strategies in competition analysis.

JCPDiscount now or later? The effect of payment framing on consumer preferences for discount timing in periodic payments
2026-05-13
Mijin Kwon, Song Oh Yoon
AI
EN

This research investigates how discount framing affects consumer preferences for the timing of discounts in subscription services. Through four main studies using online click-through behavior and experimental designs, the authors find that consumers prefer delayed discounts when the discount is expressed in the same unit as the recurring price (e.g., '$10, $10, $5'), because this identical framing creates a perception of a coherent improving sequence, leading to lower expectations of future prices. However, when discounts are framed differently (e.g., '50% off'), consumers revert to the standard preference for immediate discounts. This work contributes to pricing and temporal discounting literature by demonstrating that payment framing can reverse the typical preference for immediate benefits through sequence-continuation inferences.

This study investigates why AI-generated music achieves market success despite documented consumer aversion to AI creativity. JMEXPRESS: Cushioning the Blow: Reducing Customer Attrition in Response to Price Increase Notifications
2026-05-11
Hoorsana Damavandi, Kersi D. Antia, Praveen K. Kopalle
AI
EN

This research investigates which type of price increase justification (cost, market, or quality) most effectively reduces customer attrition. Through a randomized field experiment with 1,626 customers of a Canadian storage provider and two follow-up online scenario experiments, the authors found that market justification resulted in the lowest customer attrition—contrary to prior practitioner recommendations and academic research. This study contributes to pricing literature by providing empirical evidence on the comparative effectiveness of justification types and identifying the underlying mechanism through switching costs and fairness perceptions.

JCPEffectively communicating uncertainty: The persuasive impact of different types of hedges
2026-05-11
Demi Oba, Jonah Berger, Reihane Boghrati
AI
EN

This study examines how different types of hedging language (e.g., 'might,' 'probably,' 'I think') affect persuasion in consumer contexts. Using a multi-method approach combining experiments with machine learning analysis of millions of online reviews, the researchers found that hedges with personal perspective (vs. general) and those suggesting higher likelihood are more persuasive. The underlying mechanism is perceived confidence—personal and high-likelihood hedges signal greater communicator confidence, enhancing persuasion. The research contributes to marketing language literature by showing that hedging can protect communicators from backlash while maintaining persuasive impact, and that brands can leverage these effects through anthropomorphized agents.

P&MThe AI Agent in the Room: Rethinking Consumer Decision‐Making in the Age of Autonomous AI
2026-05-11
Anil Bilgihan, Massimiliano Ostinelli, Melanie Lorenz, Ye Zhang
AI
EN

This editorial addresses the lack of a shared definition and theoretical framework for AI agents (AIAs) in marketing scholarship. The authors propose a definition of AIAs based on three features (autonomy, adaptive learning/memory, and tool-enabled connectivity), introduce a typology distinguishing advisory from transactional AIAs, and present a three-stage framework of consumer decision-making with AIAs (prompting, processing, and response) incorporating relational capital and AI learning feedback loops. Through systematic mapping of AIA-focused articles in leading marketing journals (2020-2026), they reveal that existing research heavily focuses on consumer responses while neglecting prompting behavior, agent cognition, privacy, transparency, and relational dynamics. The paper argues that transactional AIAs represent a paradigm shift where consumers are no longer sole cognitive actors and preferences are co-constructed through human-agent conversation, outlining a research agenda to address these gaps.

P&MExternalizing the Mental Struggle: How Anthropomorphism Enhances Health Engagement via Emotional Detachment and Self‐Efficacy
2026-05-11
Jiancai Liao, Shoujiang Zhou, Khloé Qi Kang
AI
EN

This research investigates how anthropomorphism can enhance engagement with mental health communications, addressing the unique challenge that mental disorders create a sense of self-illness fusion unlike physical illnesses. Through four studies (N = 1328), the authors demonstrate that anthropomorphizing mental disorders as independent, human-like entities promotes protective behaviors through a coping appraisal pathway: emotional detachment leads to enhanced self-efficacy, which in turn increases health engagement. The effect is specific to independent (not dependent) anthropomorphism and is attenuated among individuals who hold natural kind beliefs viewing mental disorders as biologically fixed. This research contributes to marketing theory by identifying a novel mechanism distinct from threat appraisal pathways previously established for physical diseases.

P&MBolstering Heroism in Crisis Communications: How Communicating Employees' Performance can Improve Consumers' Responses
2026-05-06
I. Baghi, Ilaria Baghi
AI
EN

This study investigates whether attributing heroism to employees in crisis communications can enhance consumer responses following external crises such as weather disruptions or cyberattacks. Using five experiments, the research develops a moderated mediation model showing that heroism-framed recovery messages improve positive word of mouth, purchase intentions, and donation behavior, with these effects mediated by admiration toward employees. This research contributes to marketing literature by demonstrating the strategic value of heroism attributions in crisis communications.

P&MVisual Implied Motion in Marketing: A Dual‐Route Framework of Perceptual Persuasion
2026-05-06
Tianyi Zhang, Charles Spence
AI
EN

This systematic review examines how implied motion (depicted movement in static images) influences consumer persuasion in marketing contexts. By synthesizing 31 experimental studies within the Elaboration Likelihood Model framework, the authors identify a dual-route mechanism: implied motion enhances perceptual salience and attention, then operates either through central-route elaboration (via mental simulation and narrative transportation) or peripheral-route processing (triggering heuristic inferences like energy and freshness). The effects are moderated by consumer-level factors (consumption goals, involvement) and design-level factors (product type, brand-motion fit). This review provides the first theoretically integrated framework explaining how dynamic cues in static visuals shape consumer attitudes, purchase intentions, and willingness to pay.

JA

本論文は、パッケージ、ロゴ、広告などの静止画像における「暗示的動き（implied motion）」が消費者の説得にどのように影響するかを体系的にレビューしたものである。31件の実験研究を精緻化見込みモデル（ELM）の枠組みで統合し、暗示的動きが知覚的顕著性と注意を高めた後、中心ルート（メンタルシミュレーションや物語への没入を通じた精緻化）または周辺ルート（エネルギーや新鮮さなどのヒューリスティック推論）という二重経路で作用することを明らかにした。これらの効果は消費者要因（消費目的、関与度）とデザイン要因（製品タイプ、ブランドと動きの適合性）により調整される。本レビューは、静止画像内の動的手がかりが消費者の態度や購買意図を形成するメカニズムについて、初めて理論的に統合された枠組みを提供している。

P&MNarrative Positioning of a Fake Human: The Credibility of Virtual Influencers in Their Communication Styles
2026-05-05
Karina Sokolova, Charles Pérez, Ekaterina Besson
AI
EN

This research investigates how communication styles, specifically pronoun usage, affect the credibility and persuasiveness of virtual influencers compared to human influencers. Study 1 analyzed 391 virtual influencers on X, identifying four pronoun-based communication styles (agent-centered, group-centered, audience-centered, and informational), while Study 2 experimentally examined how self- versus audience-centered messaging affects audience perceptions in fitness Instagram posts. The findings reveal that audience-centered messages enhance perceptions of anthropomorphism for virtual influencers, which in turn increases credibility and self-efficacy, ultimately predicting exercise intentions. This research contributes to influencer marketing literature by demonstrating that pronoun-based communication style indirectly affects persuasion outcomes through anthropomorphism, suggesting virtual influencers' potential for both marketing and health promotion.

P&MEmotional Showdown: Can Virtual Endorsers Out‐Express Human Endorsers in Digital Advertising?
2026-05-05
Chenying Hai, Jun Yan, Yinghao He, Yinglu Wu
AI
EN

This study investigates whether virtual endorsers can convey positive emotions as effectively as human endorsers in digital advertising. Using field data analysis with a large language model and five controlled experiments, the research found that human endorsers outperform virtual endorsers in conveying abstract emotions (e.g., hope, gratitude), while no significant difference exists for concrete emotions (e.g., happiness, excitement). Emotional contagion and perceived expectation violation serve as parallel mediators, and perceived self-endorser attitude similarity can mitigate virtual endorsers' disadvantage with abstract emotions. These findings contribute to understanding emotional dynamics in endorser marketing and offer practical guidance for selecting endorsers based on emotion type.

P&MWhen and How Does Generative AI Outperform Human Agents in Service Recovery?
2026-05-05
Hesam Olya, Amin Nazifi, Babak Taheri, Huaiyu Li
AIartificial intelligence
EN

This study investigates when and how generative AI (GenAI) outperforms human agents in service failure and recovery contexts. Using qualitative interviews, a lab experiment, and four online experiments in live streaming settings, the research applies Cognitive Appraisal Theory to compare GenAI and human agents across single and double deviation scenarios. Key findings reveal that GenAI leads to higher repurchase intention after single failures, while human agents perform better after double deviations; however, high empathy equalizes outcomes and compensation enhances GenAI's effectiveness beyond human agents, with promotion emotion mediating these effects. The study contributes actionable guidance for practitioners on strategically deploying GenAI versus human agents based on failure complexity.

本研究は、サービス失敗と回復（SFR）の文脈において、生成AI（GenAI）が人間のエージェントをいつ、どのように上回るかを検討している。質的インタビュー、ラボ実験、ライブストリーミング環境での4つのオンライン実験を用い、認知的評価理論（CAT）に基づいて単一失敗と二重逸脱シナリオにおけるGenAIと人間エージェントの効果を比較した。主要な発見として、単一失敗後はGenAIがより高い再購買意図をもたらす一方、二重逸脱後は人間エージェントが優れるが、高い共感性はGenAIの成果を人間エージェントと同等にし、補償はGenAIの回復成果を人間エージェント以上に高めることが示され、これらの効果はプロモーション感情によって媒介される。本研究は、失敗の複雑さに応じてGenAIと人間エージェントを戦略的に使い分けるための実務的指針を提供している。

JCRMade With AI: Consumer Engagement with Social Media Containing AI Disclosures
2026-05-05
Stephan Carney, Ignacio Riveros, Stephanie Tully
AIartificial intelligence
EN

This research investigates how AI-generated content (AIGC) disclosures on social media affect consumer engagement. Using analysis of TikTok engagement data following their AIGC disclosure policy introduction and eight preregistered experiments, the authors find that AIGC disclosures reduce consumer engagement. Crucially, this reduction is not driven by concerns about content quality or AI aversion, but rather by diminished parasocial connection—the one-sided emotional bonds between consumers and creators—mediated by perceived creator effort. The study contributes a novel mechanism explaining consumer responses to AI disclosures and offers practical implications for platform policies and disclosure design, suggesting that signals of greater creator effort can mitigate engagement losses.

P&MInside the Quiet Luxury Mindset: How Do Self‐Related Orientations Influence Inconspicuous Luxury Consumption?
2026-05-04
Xiaoyu Zhang, Eugene Cheng-Xi Aw, Ian Phau
AI
EN

This study investigates how self-related orientations (self-monitoring, self-directed pleasure, and self-identity) influence inconspicuous luxury consumption. Using a mixed-methods approach combining PLS-SEM, fsQCA, and qualitative interviews with Chinese luxury consumers, the research found that association with elite and privacy are primary motivations for inconspicuous luxury consumption, with self-monitoring enhancing elite association, self-directed pleasure fostering privacy motivation, and self-identity reinforcing both. The qualitative findings further revealed that inconspicuous luxury consumption emerges from the interplay of social structure, psychological motivation, and behavioral strategies guided by cultural capital and self-oriented aesthetics. This study contributes to luxury consumption literature by revealing the mechanism through which self-related orientations drive inconspicuous consumption and offers practical implications for luxury brand marketing strategies.

P&MCompanionship as Service: The Psychological Efficacy of Commercialized Interactions in Reducing Loneliness
2026-05-02
Chen Wanyi, Ruizhi Yuan, Martin J. Liu
AI
EN

This study investigated whether paid companionship services can effectively reduce loneliness and through what psychological mechanisms they operate. Using a mixed-methods design combining qualitative exploration (Study 1) and two experiments (Study 2), the researchers found that paid companionship significantly reduced immediate loneliness compared to control conditions, with this effect statistically mediated by enhanced state social connection and moderated by social presence (specifically, out-group audience presence amplified the effect). The research also identified potential long-term challenges including emotional dependency and diminishing marginal utility. This study provides the first empirical evidence for the psychological efficacy of commercialized companionship, contributing a novel conceptualization of market-mediated relational exchange to service literature.

P&MEliciting Mental Time Travel Through Digital Product Displays and Its Positive Influence on Purchase‐Oriented Outcomes
2026-05-02
Jennifer Brannon Barhorst, Nina Krey, Graeme McLean, Heiner Evanschitzky
AI
EN

This study investigates how digital product displays elicit mental time travel (MTT) to the past and future, and how this cognitive process influences purchase-oriented outcomes including inspiration to purchase, booking intention, and willingness to pay. Using three experimental studies across different product categories (snack food, cruise travel, and wine), the authors demonstrate that past MTT positively influences future MTT, which in turn enhances all three purchase outcomes. The research also reveals that immersion does not moderate these relationships, while habitual fiction engagement (brain plasticity) strengthens the effect of future MTT on willingness to pay. This work contributes to marketing literature by introducing mental time travel as a novel mechanism explaining how digital product displays drive consumer purchase behavior.

P&MBrand Consumption Denial: Conceptualization, Scale Development, and Validation
2026-05-02
Ulun Akturan, Nilşah Cavdar Aksoy, Deniz Kuter
AI
EN

This study introduces and conceptualizes 'brand consumption denial,' a novel construct describing consumers who avoid brands publicly while continuing to use them privately. Through five studies, the authors developed and validated a multidimensional scale measuring two dimensions: social judgment–driven and secret love–driven brand consumption denial. Key findings reveal these dimensions have divergent effects on consumer-brand relationships, with social judgment–driven denial weakening brand attachment and recommendation intention, while secret love–driven denial sustains attachment and reinforces recommendation intention without self-disclosure. This research contributes to brand relationship literature by addressing an understudied phenomenon and providing a reliable measurement tool for future research.

JMEXPRESS: Beyond Visibility: The Disability Inclusion Effect in Advertising
2026-04-24
Martina Cossu, Zachary Estes, Joachim Vosgerau
AI
EN

This research investigates how brands can effectively include people with disabilities in advertising beyond mere visibility. Through nine preregistered studies examining various products, endorsers, and disability types, the authors demonstrate a robust positive effect of disability inclusion on consumer attitudes toward ads, brands, and products. The effect emerges because consumers perceive brands as supporting societal integration of people with disabilities, regardless of whether inclusion is voluntary or regulatory-mandated. P&MPartitioned or All‐Inclusive Pricing? It Depends on the Brand–Customer Relationship Type
2026-04-22
Hsin‐Hsien Liu, Hsuan‐Yi Chou, Xin‐Yi Chen
AI
EN

This study investigates how the effectiveness of partitioned pricing versus all-inclusive pricing differs depending on whether customers have communal or exchange relationships with brands. P&MDual Pathways of Loneliness in the Marketplace: Emotional Attachment, Empowerment, and Evaluations of Brand Warmth and Brand Competence
2026-04-22
Aulona Ulqinaku, Gülen Sarial‐Abi, Selma Kadić‐Maglajlić
AI
EN

This research investigates how different types of loneliness affect consumer preferences for warm versus competent brands. Using six studies including a national field study, incentive-aligned choice experiments, longitudinal brand-level panel data, and online experiments, the authors demonstrate that individual loneliness (personal social disconnection) activates agency-restoration motives, leading to preferences for competent brands, while collective loneliness (shared disconnection at the context level) triggers emotional attachment and preferences for warm brands. The key contribution is establishing that loneliness operates through dual pathways depending on the referent level, resolving prior inconsistencies in the literature. Managerially, firms can enhance message effectiveness by matching competence- or warmth-focused communications to observable social contexts.

P&MDecoding Consumer–AI Interaction Through Perceived Cognitive Capability and Anthropomorphism: Evidence From Unstructured Big Data
2026-04-21
S. R. Nikhashemi, Levent Altinay
AIartificial intelligence
EN

This study investigates how consumers interpret and evaluate AI voice assistants in everyday interactions, focusing on perceived cognitive capability and anthropomorphism. Using unsupervised machine learning to analyze 150,020 Reddit posts from UK users, the researchers identified seven key topics capturing consumer evaluation and conducted sentiment-stratified topic co-occurrence analysis. The key finding reveals a semantic asymmetry where intelligence-related attributes are interpreted positively as learning and responsiveness in favorable contexts but shift toward surveillance concerns in unfavorable contexts. This research contributes a theory-building explanation of consumer-AI interaction and demonstrates that consumer evaluations emerge from configurational patterns of interacting attributes rather than isolated factors.

2026-04-17

AI

JCRBrief Commentary: Theory Testing for Differences in Process – Hypothesizing, Testing, and Reporting Comparisons Between Indirect Effects
2026-04-16
Romain Cadario, Dan Schley, Gavan J. Fitzsimons
AI
EN

This paper addresses how consumer researchers should properly map theoretical hypotheses about process differences onto statistical tests when comparing indirect effects across contexts (moderated mediation) or mediators (parallel mediation). The authors demonstrate that different theoretical claims require different statistical approaches—for instance, claiming one conditional indirect effect is greater than another requires testing the index of moderated mediation, not simply reporting significance patterns. P&MFraming the Feed: How Visual Elements Shape Manipulative Intent Inferences and Consumer Responses to In‐Feed Ads on Social Media
2026-04-16
Patricia Rossi, Felipe Pantoja, Philippe Odou
AI
EN

This research investigates how visual elements in social media in-feed advertisements influence consumer resistance and brand responses. JCRA Framework for Understanding Consumer Response to the Depiction of Historically Underrepresented Identities in Marketing Communications
2026-04-14
Cory Haltman, Jianna Jin, Grant E Donnelly, Rebecca Walker Reczek
AI
EN

This research investigates why consumer responses to historically underrepresented identities (HUIs) in marketing communications vary, ranging from backlash to acceptance. Using moral foundations theory, the authors develop a framework proposing that while liberals respond positively to all HUI depictions due to their emphasis on Fairness, conservatives' responses depend on two dimensions: perceived agency and normativity regarding bodily purity. The findings reveal that conservatives react negatively only to HUIs perceived as high on both dimensions (e.g., obese, transgender, or hijab-wearing models), and this negative response is attenuated when the identity is low on either dimension or when HUIs appear as a numerical minority in campaigns. This framework contributes to understanding political ideology's role in advertising effectiveness and offers practical guidance for inclusive marketing strategies.

JCRRepair Service Signals: How Brand Repair Services Signal Unused Utility and Increase Product Repair
2026-04-13
Nathan Allred, Karen Page Winterich
AI
EN

This research investigates why brand repair services, compared to third-party services, are more effective at promoting product repair over replacement. Through two field studies and four lab experiments, the authors demonstrate that brand and brand-certified repair services signal unused utility in malfunctioning products, thereby increasing consumers' likelihood to repair rather than replace. The effect diminishes when consumers have domain expertise, when products hold sentimental value (as repair occurs without brand signals), or when product upgrades are salient (which attenuates the unused utility signal). This work contributes to signaling theory, unused utility literature, and offers practical implications for sustainability efforts and brand management strategies.

JMREXPRESS: Separating the Artist from the Art: Social Media Boycotts, Platform Sanctions, and Music Consumption
2026-04-13
Daniel Winkler, Nils Wlömert, Jūra Liaukonytė
AI
EN

This paper examines how consumer demand for an artist's music changes when social media campaigns call for boycotts following misconduct. Using R. Kelly's case and an identification strategy that leverages variation in song-removal status and geographic demand, the authors distinguish between supply-side (platform visibility) and demand-side (intentional boycott) effects. The key finding reveals that decreased music consumption is primarily driven by reduced platform visibility through playlist removal rather than intentional consumer boycotts, and media coverage actually has promotional effects on demand. 2026-04-13
Xiaoyan (Jenny) Liu, Chi Hoang, Sharon Ng
AIjoint consumption
EN

This research investigates whether AI-driven autonomous stores affect consumers' prosocial behavior compared to human-operated stores. Through nine online and field experiments, the authors find that consumers engage in less prosocial behavior after interacting with autonomous stores, an effect driven by reduced social connectedness due to the absence of human interaction at service touchpoints. JMEXPRESS: The ABCs of Marketing Principles: A Principles-Based Framework for Marketing Alignment and Adaptation
2026-04-13
Molly R. Burchett, Brian Murtha, Bernard J. Jaworski
AI
EN

This study examines how marketing principles function within organizations and how they can be effectively implemented to balance alignment and adaptation. Based on 38 in-depth interviews with marketing professionals across various firms and industries, the authors develop the 'ABC' framework comprising Articulating, Bridging, and Calibrating principles. The research reveals that less specific principles enable flexibility but risk misalignment costs (resource misallocation, mental burden, marketing sprawl), while more specific principles provide clarity but risk maladaptation costs (staleness, reduced professional development, opportunity costs). JMEXPRESS: Climate Communications in IPOs: Unpacking the Influence of Climate Disclosure Volume, Sender, and Message Characteristics
2026-04-13
Ankit Anand, Alok R. Saboo, Ritesh Adhyapak
AI
EN

This study examines how climate disclosure volume in IPO prospectuses affects firm performance, proposing and testing a U-shaped relationship grounded in damage-limitation logic. Using data from 1,586 IPO firms and BERT-based language models to analyze climate-related text, the authors find that low to moderate disclosure levels harm valuations by amplifying risk salience, while higher levels generate benefits through enhanced reputation and information transparency. The research further reveals that sender characteristics (underwriter reputation, customer concentration, market orientation) and message characteristics (discretionary disclosure, message clarity) moderate this relationship, and that climate risk-management disclosures positively impact valuations while pure risk disclosures damage them. This study contributes to marketing and finance literature by providing nuanced insights into optimal climate communication strategies for IPO firms and policymakers.

P&MRiding Solo: The Impact of Relationship Status on Brand Attachment
2026-04-10
Rui Chen, Kunyu Wu, Zhaoyan Shang
AI
EN

This research examines how being single affects consumers' brand attachment, proposing attachment avoidance as the mediating mechanism. Across five studies conducted in China and the United States with 2,015 participants, the authors found that single individuals exhibit weaker brand attachment than non-singles because singlehood elevates attachment avoidance, which subsequently reduces affiliative connections with brands. The study identifies boundary conditions including secure-attachment priming, materialism, and cultural context (individualistic vs. family-centered societies) that moderate this effect. Theoretically, this work contributes by demonstrating an assimilation process rather than compensatory attachment, extending attachment theory from interpersonal relationships to consumer-brand relationships.

This research investigates how God-related cues influence consumer food choices, specifically preferences for ultra-processed foods. Through six studies including a field experiment, the authors find that high God salience increases consumers' preference for ultra-processed foods over unprocessed alternatives. This effect is mediated by perceived healing by God—consumers believe God will mitigate negative health consequences—and is moderated by the perceived predictability of God's intervention, with the effect emerging only when intervention is seen as predictable. The findings contribute to compensatory control theory and the literature on religion and consumer decision-making, offering implications for promoting healthier food choices.

JA

本研究は、神に関連する手がかり（God salience）が消費者の食品選択、特に超加工食品への選好にどのような影響を与えるかを検討している。フィールド実験を含む6つの研究を通じて、神の存在が顕著な状況では、消費者が未加工食品よりも超加工食品を選択する傾向が高まることを明らかにした。この効果は「神による癒し」の知覚によって媒介され、消費者は神が超加工食品摂取による健康への悪影響を軽減してくれると信じる傾向がある。さらに、神の介入が予測可能と認識される場合にのみこの効果が生じることが示された。本研究は補償的コントロール理論および宗教と消費者意思決定に関する研究に貢献し、健康的な食品選択を促進するための実践的示唆を提供している。

P&MSurprise Marketing
2026-04-09
Xin‐Jean Lim, Weng Marc Lim, Park Thaichon
AI
EN

This article addresses the fragmented scholarly understanding of surprise marketing by introducing the PIEL framework, which integrates four core tenets: Probabilistic outcomes, Intentional design, Exclusive ownership, and Low stake offerings. Through comprehensive analysis of contemporary marketing studies and marketplace practices, the authors examine the evolution of surprise marketing across three strategic phases (emergence, engagement, and immersion) in response to phygital trends and changing consumer expectations. The key finding reveals a duality in surprise marketing: while it evokes powerful positive emotions, it can paradoxically heighten consumer vulnerability, skepticism, and perceived unfairness. The article contributes by identifying critical research gaps and proposing future directions including social change applications, inclusivity, ethical concerns around dynamic pricing, consumer wellbeing, and paradoxical emotions.

JCRWhen Goods Were Odds: Do People Prefer Uncertain Goods After Uncertainty Is Resolved?
2026-04-08
Beidi Hu, Siyuan Yin, Alice Moon
AI
EN

This research investigates how people evaluate goods that originated from uncertain prospects (e.g., raffle prizes) after the uncertainty has been resolved. Through seven experiments with over 12,000 participants, the authors demonstrate an 'uncertainty spillover effect' whereby people prefer goods that came from uncertain origins compared to identical goods that were always known. The effect is explained by downward counterfactual thinking—uncertainty makes people perceive outcomes as superior relative to worse alternatives that could have occurred. This research contributes to consumer behavior literature by showing that uncertainty's influence persists beyond its resolution, affecting product evaluations and extending to associated products.

JA

本研究は、不確実な状況（例：抽選）から得られた商品が、不確実性が解消された後にどのように評価されるかを検討した。12,000人以上を対象とした7つの実験を通じて、「不確実性スピルオーバー効果」を実証し、不確実な起源を持つ商品は、最初から確定していた同一商品よりも好まれることを明らかにした。この効果は下方反事実思考によって説明され、不確実性により「起こりえたより悪い結果」と比較して現在の結果が優れていると知覚される。本研究は、不確実性の影響がその解消後も持続し、商品評価や関連商品にまで波及することを示し、消費者行動研究に貢献している。

JMREXPRESS: Does Puffery Sell? Evidence from Airbnb
2026-04-08
Michael Thomas
AI
EN

This study investigates whether puffery (positive but vague quality claims) influences consumer behavior on Airbnb. Using a hazard model that exploits within-listing changes to hosts' property descriptions, the research finds that adding a single puffed claim increases bookings by approximately 0.7 days per year, with minimal negative impact on ratings or review sentiment (less than 0.03 standard deviations). The findings challenge the legal assumption that consumers adequately discount puffed claims while supporting continued legal protection since puffery does not reduce consumer satisfaction. This research contributes to marketing and consumer protection literature by providing causal evidence on puffery's effectiveness in a real marketplace setting.

JMEXPRESS: Human or AI? Identity Salience Enhances Historically Marginalized (But Not Privileged) Consumers’ Choice of Algorithmic Service Providers
2026-04-08

AI
EN

This research investigates how identity salience affects consumers from historically marginalized versus privileged groups in their choice of algorithmic service providers (e.g., AI chatbots, robots). Through field studies and controlled experiments, the authors find that when identity is salient, consumers from historically marginalized groups (e.g., Black individuals) prefer algorithmic service providers due to concerns about identity-based social judgment, while this effect does not occur for privileged consumers. P&MOn the Transformative Nature of Luxury Consumption and Consumer Well‐Being: A Systematic Literature Review and Research Agenda
2026-04-04
Solon Magrizos, Maria C. Voutsa, Minas N. Kastanakis
AI
EN

This systematic literature review examines how and why luxury consumption contributes to consumer well-being, synthesizing insights from 109 academic articles. The study reveals that traditional/masstige luxury primarily delivers temporal hedonic gratification, while unconventional luxury (experiential, ethical, sustainable) aligns more closely with eudaimonic well-being. JMEXPRESS: Extended Reality in Business-to-Business Sales: Performance Implications, Adoption Drivers, and Contingency Effects
2026-04-02
Janina Mettler, Aline Isabelle Lanzrath, Christian Homburg
AI
EN

This study investigates when, why, and how extended reality (XR) technologies create value in business-to-business (B2B) sales contexts. Using a multimethod approach including quasi-experimental field data, cross-industry surveys, and between-subjects experiments with both salespeople and buyers, the authors find that XR improves buyer- and seller-related outcomes (e.g., loyalty, upselling) by enhancing customers' mental imagery ability. P&MReal vs. Imagined Sensory Inputs in Virtual Reality: A Construal‐Level Based Conceptual Framework and Future Research Directions

2026-03-31

AI
EN

This research examines how individual differences in managing multiple identities (identity integration) moderate the processing of social information in consumption choices. P&MHow Downsizing a Typical Product at a Stable Unit Price Recalibrates Price Perceptions: A Contrast–Assimilation Account of Perceived Category and New‐Product Expensiveness
2026-03-31
Frank Goedertier, Karen Gorissen, Bert Weijters
AI
EN

This research investigates how downsizing a typical product in an assortment while maintaining a stable unit price affects consumers' perceived expensiveness of the category and newly introduced products. Through three experimental studies (N=447, 476, 294) using visual scenario-based online surveys with FMCG products (chocolate and soft drinks), the authors demonstrate that this tactic reduces perceived expensiveness of the overall category assortment but simultaneously increases perceived expensiveness of new products within that assortment. These opposing effects are explained through contrast-assimilation psychological mechanisms, with assortment size identified as a moderator and unit price disclosure attenuating these effects. The study contributes to reference price research and provides retailers with a margin-preserving alternative to steep discounting for managing price perceptions.

Vik Naidoo
AIartificial intelligence
EN

This paper investigates how consumers respond to carbon-transparency cues that reveal the environmental costs of AI-enabled services, addressing the ethical dilemma between digital convenience and ecological sustainability. EN

This study examines how organizations can transform their new product development (NPD) processes to create inclusive products that address the needs of edge consumers—those who are underserved or unserved in the marketplace. JMREXPRESS: Vocal Similarity, Timbre, and Persuasion in Consumer-Spokesperson Interactions
2026-03-25
Na Kyong Hyun, Michael Lowe, Aradhna Krishna
AI
EN

This study investigates whether similarity in vocal timbre between consumers and spokespersons influences persuasion. Using machine learning to measure vocal similarity through mel-frequency cepstral coefficients (MFCCs), the researchers analyzed 7,002 Shark Tank entrepreneur-investor interactions and 2,091 Kickstarter campaigns, complemented by four laboratory experiments. Results demonstrate that vocal similarity increases persuasion, investment likelihood, and fundraising success, with effects mediated by increased trust in spokesperson competence and attenuated when external credibility signals are present. The research contributes new vocal analytics tools and extends similarity-persuasion theory to the auditory domain of consumer-spokesperson interactions.

This research investigates whether company acknowledgment of customer participation in take-back programs can enhance sustainability perceptions and encourage repeat participation. Through seven studies including a field study, the authors demonstrate that simple acknowledgment increases customers' emotional attachment and sense of partnership with the company, leading to higher perceived sustainability and greater likelihood of repeat participation. The effect is stronger for take-back programs than company-beneficial programs, and is attenuated by high self-brand connection and industry greenwashing. This work contributes theoretical insights into customer-company partnerships in sustainability contexts and provides practical guidance for managers implementing take-back programs.

P&MAI Epistemic Disengagement and Consumer Dependence: An Augmentation‐Substitution Framework
2026-03-24
Vasilis Theoharakis, Nikolaos Mylonopoulos
AIartificial intelligence
EN

This study investigates how consumers justify accepting AI as a trusted reasoning source and when this acceptance preserves versus forfeits their capacity for independent thinking. Drawing on Bandura's moral disengagement theory, the authors introduce 'AI epistemic disengagement' and conduct two studies with nearly 600 US consumers to identify two distinct patterns of AI dependence: augmentation (maintaining epistemic responsibility through critical evaluation) and substitution (abdicating responsibility through uncritical acceptance). Key findings reveal that augmentation is associated with Need for Cognition, prompting skills, and more intense/habitual AI use, while substitution shows opposite patterns. The study contributes an augmentation-substitution framework that helps explain consumer AI dependence and offers practical implications for preserving consumer welfare during AI adoption.

JCRBrief Commentary: A Framework for Detecting AI Agents in Online Research
2026-03-21
Felipe M. Affonso
AI
EN

This paper addresses the emerging challenge of detecting AI agents autonomously completing online surveys, which traditional attention checks fail to identify. The author introduces the Cognitive Trap Framework, which exploits architectural constraints of vision-language models to create questions that are difficult for AI but easy for humans. Testing against 1,007 human participants and 526 AI agents showed that cognitive traps detected 97.1% of agents while only flagging 4.1% of humans, compared to just 2.3% detection with traditional methods. The framework's key contribution is its adaptability—since AI model improvements are non-monotonic, new traps can be generated as agent architectures evolve, with validated tools made publicly available for researchers.

本論文は、視覚対応AIエージェントがオンライン調査を自律的に完了できるようになった現状において、従来の注意確認手法では検出が困難であるという課題に取り組んでいる。著者は、視覚言語モデルのアーキテクチャ上の制約を利用し、AIには困難だが人間には容易な質問を設計する「認知トラップフレームワーク」を提案した。1,007名の人間参加者と526体のAIエージェントを対象とした検証では、認知トラップはエージェントの97.1%を検出し（従来手法は2.3%）、人間の誤検出率はわずか4.1%であった。本フレームワークの主要な貢献は、AIモデルの進化が非単調であるため新たなトラップを継続的に生成できる適応性にあり、検証済みのツールが研究者向けに公開されている点にある。

JCRLimiting Accessibility: How Targeting Consumers with Disabilities Constrains Acceptable Prices for Innovations
2026-03-18
Musa Essa, Johannes Boegershausen, Gabriele Paolacci
AI
EN

This research investigates how mass-market consumers react to innovations targeting people with disabilities, particularly regarding price acceptability. Through nine main studies and six supplementary studies (twelve preregistered), the authors find that consumers view price premiums as less acceptable when products target people with disabilities compared to other segments, even when cost justifications are provided. P&MDistant or Proximal? The Impact of Temporal Cues in Historical Lowest Price Claims on Consumer Purchase Behavior
2026-03-18

This research investigates how temporal cues in historical lowest price claims (distant vs. proximal) affect consumer purchase behavior. JMEXPRESS: Not Just Bad Luck: Income-Based Gaps in Lottery Losses
2026-03-16
Paul Parker, Paulo Albuquerque, Yakov Bart
AI
EN

This study investigates whether lower-income consumers face additional disadvantages in lottery play beyond spending a larger share of their income. Using market-level and transaction-level data from California, the authors find that lower-income players are less responsive to high-jackpot games, use random number generators less frequently, and favor popular number combinations—behaviors that interact with pari-mutuel prize structures to reduce their expected earnings by approximately 7% per ticket, totaling nearly $3 million annually. The research evaluates alternative game formats including fixed non-jackpot prizes, mandatory random selection, and raffle-style jackpots, finding these interventions can reduce income-based disparities by up to 60%. This work contributes to marketing theory by linking product design dimensions to consumer heterogeneity and provides actionable policy recommendations for creating more equitable lottery systems.

P&MTime for Action: Implied Action Does It!
2026-03-16
Gudrun Roose, Maggie Geuens, Cristián Buzeta
AI
EN

This research investigates whether implied-action cues in static images—visual elements suggesting ongoing activity through objects in use without showing people or motion—can enhance consumer responses. Across four studies with 1,026 participants testing various product types (airlines, rentals, televisions), the authors found that implied-action cues positively influence brand attitudes, perceived quality, purchase intentions, and information-seeking behavior compared to no cues or non-action cues. JMREXPRESS: Measuring Heterogeneity in TV Advertising Elasticities: Evidence from 135 Retail and Restaurant Brands
2026-03-12
Samsun Knight, Tsung-Yiou Hsieh, Yakov Bart
AI
EN

This study investigates the heterogeneity of TV advertising effectiveness across store characteristics and advertising levels using a large-scale panel of 135 US retail and restaurant brands. The authors find significant heterogeneity in TV advertising elasticity for over 93% of brands, yet firms' actual ad allocations follow simple heuristics rather than exploiting this heterogeneity—overinvesting in dense, high-income markets while underinvesting in areas with high concentrations of college-educated residents. The research demonstrates that identical-budget reallocations leveraging this heterogeneity could improve ad lift by a median of 2.35 percentage points and generate tens of millions in additional revenue, with 14-16% of brands with negative ROI potentially achieving positive ROI. This study contributes to advertising effectiveness literature by providing large-scale empirical evidence of suboptimal ad allocation practices and quantifying the substantial financial gains from heterogeneity-based reallocation strategies.

JMEXPRESS: The Making-Up-For-Failure Nudge: Framing Subgoals as Opportunities for Redemption Increases Goal Persistence
2026-03-12
Shannon M. Duncan, Marissa Sharif
AI
EN

This research investigates how framing subgoals as opportunities to make up for past failures affects goal persistence. Through two longitudinal real-behavior studies, four lab studies, and one hypothetical study across domains including language learning, exercise, and word search games, the authors demonstrate that a 'making-up-for-failure nudge' increases goal-consistent behavior after setbacks. P&MCustomer Experience: Heterogeneity Insights From Meta‐Analytic Synthesis
2026-03-12
Wagner Júnior Ladeira, Weng Marc Lim, Tareq Rasul, Fernando de Oliveira Santini, Raouf Ahmad Rather, et al.
AI
EN

This study addresses the overlooked issue of heterogeneity (variability) in customer experience (CX) research by conducting a meta-analytic review of 91 existing meta-analyses. Using Becker and Jaakkola's (2020) CX research classification framework, the authors identified moderators (conceptual, contextual, methodological, practical, and temporal) that explain variability across four research clusters: stimuli affecting experiences, responses to stimuli, evaluative outcomes, and customer contingencies. This research contributes to CX theory by systematically mapping sources of heterogeneity and provides practical guidance for managers and future researchers to account for contextual variations.

P&MTransparency Matters: Psychological Ownership and Trust as Mediators of Explainable Artificial Intelligence Effectiveness
2026-03-10
Suresh Malodia, Yupal Shukla, Kirti Sharma, Anil Bilgihan
AIartificial intelligence
EN

This study examines how explainable AI (XAI) design influences consumers' intention to follow AI recommendations. Through four scenario-based experiments with 1,080 participants, the researchers investigated the effects of explanation level (high vs. low detail) and explanation type (process-oriented vs. outcome-oriented) across different recommendation contexts (product vs. content). The study contributes to the literature by identifying trust and psychological ownership as key mediating mechanisms through which XAI design translates into consumer action.

P&MYou Are Not Alone: Uptrend Messaging to Promote Green Consumption
2026-03-10
Yingyu Luo, Guowei Zhu, Jing Du, Jinfeng Lu, Jing Huang
AI
EN

This study investigates how uptrend messaging—communicating that green consumption is increasing—affects consumers' green product preferences. Through five studies including a field experiment, the authors demonstrate that uptrend messaging significantly enhances green consumption preferences by fostering response efficacy, the belief that purchasing green products will lead to desired environmental outcomes. The effect is strengthened when the uptrend magnitude is high or when consumers have an interdependent self-construal, but attenuated under low magnitude or independent self-construal conditions. These findings contribute to understanding how social trend information can serve as an effective behavioral intervention to narrow the attitude-behavior gap in sustainable consumption.

P&MFail to Win: How Schadenfreude and Competitive Traits Predict Download Intention in Mobile Game Fail Ads
2026-03-10
Moon Joonhyun, Yeolib Kim, Boreum Choi, Hanbyul Choi
AI
EN

This study investigates why mobile game advertisements depicting gameplay failures are effective, focusing on schadenfreude (pleasure from others' misfortune) and individual competitiveness as psychological mechanisms. Using two vignette-based experiments (N=241 and N=247), the authors found that hypercompetitiveness predicts schadenfreude, which in turn increases download intention, while perceived game difficulty moderates this effect—reasonably challenging failures evoke more schadenfreude than extremely difficult ones. The research contributes to understanding how negative emotional responses can drive positive consumer behavior and provides practical implications for marketers to optimize fail-based advertising by calibrating difficulty levels and targeting hypercompetitive consumers.
