JCRMore Correlations Signal Causation: The Effect of Correlational Scope on Perceived Causality
2026-03-07
Yue Zhang, Gabriele Paolacci
AI
EN

This research investigates when consumers perceive statistical correlations as reflecting causal relationships in the context of big data and media reporting. Through eight preregistered studies, the authors demonstrate that a correlation (e.g., between tea drinking and bone health) is perceived as more causal when the plausible cause correlates with additional outcomes (correlational scope effect). This effect is attenuated when additional outcomes are weakly related to the focal outcome, mitigated under cause-last framing, and influences product choices. The findings contribute to understanding causal judgment by proposing category-based induction as an underlying mechanism and have implications for consumer well-being.

P&MBuilt by Love: Romantic Relationship Motives and Do‐It‐Yourself Product Consumption
2026-03-07
Ali Gohary, Astrid Nørfelt, Eugene Y. Chan
AI
EN

This study investigates how romantic relationship motives influence consumers' preferences for do-it-yourself (DIY) products from an evolutionary psychology perspective. Through four main studies and two supplementary studies (N = 3,014), the researchers found that when relationship formation motives are activated, men prefer DIY products more than women due to greater risk-taking tendencies. P&MCognitive Overload and Emotional Arousal: Why Gambling Content Marketing Appeals to Children and Resists Established Educational Interventions
2026-03-07
Raffaello Rossi, Edoardo Tozzi, Agnes C. Nairn
AI
EN

This study investigates whether gambling content marketing on social media is more emotionally appealing and less recognizable as advertising to children compared to conventional gambling ads, and whether UK school-based educational interventions can mitigate these effects. Using an experiment with 58 children aged 13-17, the researchers measured appeal through combined self-reported emotional valence and physiological arousal (electrodermal activity), alongside advertising recognition via forced-choice tasks. Results showed that gambling content marketing was four times more appealing than conventional ads, with only 7% of children correctly identifying all content marketing as advertising, while the educational intervention had negligible effects. The study contributes to understanding why cognitive-based advertising literacy programs may be ineffective against emotionally engaging gambling content and demonstrates the value of combining self-report with physiological measures in assessing advertising appeal.

P&MThe Ironic Effects of Financial Constraint‐Induced Suppression on Consumer Behavior
2026-03-06
Jane So, Miao Miao, Yuki Nishida
AI
EN

This study examines how financial constraints lead consumers to suppress thoughts about their financial difficulties and spending desires as a coping mechanism, and whether this suppression triggers ironic rebound effects. JMREXPRESS: Increasing App Engagement Behaviors via Goal-Enabling Technology Features: The Role of Goal Difficulty Dimensions
2026-03-04
Jake An, André Bonfrer, Christine Eckert
AI
EN

This study examines how the difficulty of goals selected during goal-enabling technology feature (GETF) adoption affects user engagement and retention in mobile apps. Using behavioral data from an investment app combined with hierarchical modeling, staggered synthetic control, instrumental variable regression, and a field experiment, the authors address self-selection and endogeneity concerns. P&MChatbots in Airlines: Are They Really Assisting?
2026-03-02
Pooja Kushwaha, Manoj Kumar Kamila
AIartificial intelligence
EN

This study investigates how value co-destruction processes in airline chatbot interactions contribute to customer dissatisfaction. Using a dual-method approach to analyze 6,800 records including user-generated reviews and texts, the research identifies four predominant themes: poor service quality, inadequate technology, lack of empathy, and unreliable information support. JMREXPRESS: Less is More (Natural): The Effect of Ingredient Quantity Framing on Consumer Preferences
2026-03-01
Michelle Yoosun Kim, Rachel Gershon, SYDNEY E. SCOTT, Daniella Kupor, Tianqi Chen, et al.
AI
EN

This research examines whether and how ingredient quantity information influences consumer food choices. Through two preregistered pilot studies, seven preregistered experiments, and ten supplementary experiments, the authors find that consumers generally prefer products framed as containing fewer ingredients because fewer ingredients signal less processing and greater naturalness. However, when other consumption goals such as seeking indulgence or uniqueness become salient, products with more ingredients can become preferred. This work contributes to marketing literature by revealing how ingredient quantity framing biases consumer perceptions and provides actionable guidance for marketers in product positioning.

JMEXPRESS: Do Business Professionals Exhibit Racial Bias in Strategic Marketing Decisions?
2026-03-01
Jorge Jacob, Yan Vieites
AI
EN

This research investigates whether business professionals exhibit racial bias in strategic marketing decisions involving Black consumers. Through nine studies and four replications across the United States, France, and Brazil, the authors find that lay theories linking Blackness with economic hardship lead professionals to perceive Black consumers as lower income than equally earning White consumers, resulting in stereotypes characterizing Black consumers as more price sensitive and less quality-oriented. JMEXPRESS: Filling the Void: How Competing Brands Can Capitalize on a Brand Deletion
2026-03-01
Kristopher Keller, Harald J. van Heerde
AI
EN

This study investigates which competing brands benefit from brand deletions and how marketing mix adjustments influence post-deletion gains. Using a difference-in-differences analysis of 1,046 national brand deletions across 201 US markets over 10 years, the authors find that private labels benefit most from deletions, followed by sister brands, with rival national brands gaining the least, while overall category revenue does not fully recover. Key moderating factors include price similarity with the deleted brand, the deleted brand's pre-deletion market share, and category concentration, with distribution expansion and line length extensions driving gains while price increases constrain them. The research contributes to brand portfolio management by revealing that manufacturers retain only 38% of deleted brands' revenues through sister brands, highlighting significant risks associated with brand pruning strategies.

AI
EN

2026-02-26

AI
EN

This research investigates why people are more inclined to gossip about their favorite celebrities compared to other targets, using a relational approach that contrasts parasocial relationships with balanced social relationships. 2026-02-24
Yu‐Ming Hsu
AI
EN

This study addresses how firms can implement AI-driven personalization that maximizes relevance while preserving consumer autonomy and trust. P&MSelling With Negative Emotions—Better Than None? Nonverbal Expressiveness During Sales Presentations: An Experimental Approach
2026-02-17
Sandra Pauser, Udo Wagner
AI
EN

This research investigates how salespeople's nonverbal expressiveness (facial expressions, body language, and vocal cues) affects customer responses during sales presentations, drawing on the Emotions as Social Information (EASI) model. Using innovative measurement techniques including facial recognition software, body action coding, and voice analysis, two large-scale experimental studies were conducted with videotaped sales presentations and real-time customer response tracking. Key findings reveal that expressive presentations lead to more favorable customer evaluations, with positive facial expressions and direct head orientation improving responses; notably, subtle displays of negative emotions like anger can also elicit positive responses under certain conditions. The research contributes novel insights into nonverbal communication dynamics in sales contexts and demonstrates that nonverbal expressiveness is particularly influential for hedonic (vs. utilitarian) products.

JMREXPRESS: Market Effects of Inattention: Theory and Evidence from Left-Digit Bias
2026-02-16
Andreas Kraft, Raghunath Singh Rao
AI
EN

This study examines whether and how firms can profit from consumer inattention, specifically left-digit bias in odometer readings, in the used car market. Using a dataset of millions of automobile transactions over seven years, the authors theoretically model and empirically test how dealerships exploit this cognitive bias compared to private sellers. The findings reveal that dealerships strategically transact with more left-digit-biased consumers, sell more vehicles with odometer readings just below round numbers, achieve faster sales, and extract higher profit margins. This research contributes to understanding how market intermediaries can systematically 'skim' behaviorally biased consumers and capture meaningful surplus in decentralized markets.

P&MEntrepreneurial Athlete Branding: How Signal Costliness and Consumer Skepticism Shape Trust
2026-02-16
Qingru Xu, Weilu Zhang, Nicky Lewis
AI
EN

This study investigates how different athlete-brand relationship strategies (endorsements vs. co-investment and self-branding) affect consumer trust, drawing on signaling theory. JCPThe politics of impact: How political ideology shapes perceptions of the environmental impact of individual actions
2026-02-14
Aylin Cakanlar, Katherine White, Remi Trudel
AI
EN

This research investigates how political ideology shapes perceptions of the environmental impact of individual sustainable actions. Through seven studies, the authors find that conservatives perceive their sustainable behaviors as having less positive environmental impact than liberals do, which explains their lower engagement in such behaviors. This effect stems not only from climate change beliefs but also from the lower observed prevalence of sustainable behaviors within conservatives' in-groups. The research demonstrates that framing sustainable actions in health-related terms, emphasizing in-group prevalence, or explicitly communicating impact can increase conservatives' perceived impact and willingness to engage in sustainable behaviors, offering practical strategies for promoting sustainability across the political spectrum.

P&MThe Rise of Human–Computer Integration in Marketing: A Theory Synthesis
2026-02-13
Carlos Velasco, Kosuke Motoki, Olivia Petit, Aram N. Andersen, Marzieh Alaei, et al.
AI
EN

This article addresses how human–computer integration (HCInt) technologies—ranging from wearables to brain–computer implants—transform consumer experience by merging bodily, cognitive, and sensory functions with computational processes. Using a theory synthesis approach that integrates insights from human–computer interaction, psychology, and marketing, the authors develop a marketing-oriented conceptual model distinguishing between fusion (technology as bodily extension) and symbiosis (adaptive human–AI co-regulation). P&MHow to Conduct a Multi‐Domain Systematic (Literature) Review? Guidelines Using The Lotus Protocol
2026-02-10
Bart J. A. van Bueren, Weng Marc Lim, Naveen Donthu, Kevin Argus, Julian M. Water‐Lynch, et al.
AI
EN

This article addresses the challenge of conducting systematic literature reviews across multiple intersecting knowledge domains, as existing protocols typically limit researchers to single-domain or single-intersection reviews. The authors introduce The Lotus Protocol, a systematic multi-domain approach visualized through Venn diagram-like petal structures, consisting of three phases: conceptual exploration, corpora creation, and contribution capture. Demonstrated through a review synthesizing food waste, behavioral change, mobile applications, and revenue domains, the protocol identified four value propositions for food-waste-fighting apps (brokering, delivery, inventory planning, and education) while revealing theoretical gaps regarding planetary boundaries and behavioral science. The Lotus Protocol contributes a formalized methodology for synthesizing evidence across multiple knowledge domains, positioning systematic reviews as translational research capable of generating holistic insights for addressing complex, multidisciplinary grand challenges.

EN

This article examines whether AI-driven marketing advances or hinders diversity, equity, and inclusion (DEI) in the marketplace. Through a synthesis of existing research, the authors develop an integrative framework organized around three transformational themes: from design to practice, from implementation to integration, and from evaluation to empowerment. P&MDigital Cognition in Predictive Marketing Personalization: A Conceptual Framework
2026-02-06
José Ramón Saura, Vanessa Ratten, Veljko Jeremić
AIartificial intelligence
EN

This study addresses the lack of an integrative theoretical framework explaining how predictive personalization recursively interacts with consumer cognition in digital marketing. Using systematic literature review and qualitative synthesis, the authors develop a conceptual framework that models digital cognition as a closed-loop system where behavioral data feeds AI-driven algorithms that reshape users' attention, memory, and decision processes. P&MSounds Sweet: Sound Reduplication in Brand Names Enhances Sweet Taste Expectations
2026-01-31

AI
EN

This study investigates how sound reduplication in brand names affects consumers' sweet taste expectations. JCPFrom decision patience to process patience: A decision–process integration of the choice to wait and the experience of waiting
2026-01-28
Selin A. Malkoc
AI
EN

This commentary addresses the gap between two conceptualizations of consumer impatience: decision patience (choosing to wait for delayed rewards in intertemporal choice) and process patience (the emotional experience during waiting). Through theoretical integration, the author maps psychological drivers of intertemporal choice—including affective urges, cognitive representations, construal, subjective time perception, and future-self connectedness—onto Sweeny's Process Model of Patience. The key contribution is demonstrating where these two perspectives converge and diverge, proposing testable predictions about how choice patience and process patience can dissociate and how environmental cues may reduce uncertainty at the decision point while increasing impatience during the wait. This integration offers a more complete temporal model of impatience spanning from pre-decision through the entire waiting period.

JMEXPRESS: THE BRAND BACKSTORY AND THE STRATEGIC PERFORMANCE OF TRANSPARENCY
2026-01-27

AI
EN

This research examines how brands strategically balance transparency and control during backstory performances such as factory tours and visitor centers. JMEXPRESS: Endorsement Rate in Influencer Marketing
2026-01-27
Yijing Li, Angela Yi Gao, Flora F. Gu, Fine F. Leung
AI
EN

This study investigates how influencers' endorsement rates—the proportion of sponsored posts to total posts—affect consumer engagement with sponsored content. Using a multimethod approach including two field studies and two controlled experiments across platforms like Instagram, Twitter, and Douyin, the research reveals a U-shaped relationship between endorsement rate and consumer engagement. This pattern emerges from two opposing forces: higher endorsement rates increase perceived brand recognition but also raise audience suspicion of manipulative intent. The findings contribute to influencer marketing literature by identifying organic product mentions and consistent brand endorsements as moderators, while providing marketers with a practical framework for influencer selection.

Journal of Consumer Psychology
Three strategies for claiming research progress
2026-06-18
David Gal, Bobby Calder, Brian Sternthal
AI
EN

This paper addresses how consumer researchers can justify claims of research progress by identifying three distinct strategies: establishing-an-effect (demonstrating replicable X-Y relationships), theory-of-the-effect (specifying local mechanistic accounts), and theoretical framework (developing explanations transcending single effects). Through coding recent articles in leading consumer research journals, the authors find that the field overwhelmingly relies on the theory-of-the-effect strategy while rarely advancing theoretical frameworks. The paper clarifies the core logic, methodological emphases, and vulnerabilities of each strategy, and provides actionable guidance for progressing from theories of effects to broader frameworks. The contribution lies in arguing that deliberately combining these strategies provides a stronger foundation for claiming meaningful research progress.

本論文は、消費者研究者が研究の進歩をどのような根拠で主張できるかという問いに対し、3つの戦略を提示している。具体的には、効果の確立（変数XとYの関係の再現性）、効果の理論（局所的なメカニズムの特定）、理論的フレームワーク（単一の効果を超えた説明）である。主要な消費者研究ジャーナルの最近の論文をコーディング分析した結果、当該分野は効果の理論戦略に圧倒的に依存しており、理論的フレームワークの構築はほとんど行われていないことが明らかになった。各戦略の論理・方法論的重点・脆弱性を明確化し、効果の理論からフレームワークへ発展させるための実践的指針を提供することで、3つの戦略を意図的に組み合わせることが研究進歩の主張をより強固にすると論じている。

Gender identity, overconfidence, and risk attitudes
2026-06-16
Edward R. Lawrence, Benedikt Wick
AI
EN

This study investigates the relationship between gender identity, overconfidence, and investment risk-taking behaviors using a measure of overconfidence defined as the gap between perceived and actual financial knowledge. The empirical analysis reveals that nonbinary individuals exhibit significantly lower levels of financial overconfidence compared to those identifying as male or female, attributable to more modest self-assessment rather than differences in actual knowledge. The findings show that higher overconfidence correlates with greater willingness to take financial risks, and nonbinary individuals demonstrate considerably lower risk-taking inclination in investment decisions. This research contributes to the literature by extending the analysis of gender effects in financial decision-making beyond the traditional binary framework and ruling out mental health challenges and generic minority effects as explanations.

The continuity heuristic: Temporal extrapolation in consumer judgment
2026-06-14
Matthew Fisher, Adam H. Smiley
AI
EN

This research investigates how consumers predict the future longevity of products, brands, and services. Across 24 studies with 9,718 participants, the authors demonstrate the 'continuity heuristic'—a mental shortcut where consumers use past duration as a basis for forecasting future continuation. The effect persists across diverse domains including brand heritage, product obsolescence, subscriptions, and trends, even when quality is controlled and age is not explicitly mentioned. The authors propose a question-substitution mechanism and show the effect weakens for technology products (with clear lifespan norms) and expert consumers, contributing to temporal judgment psychology and offering marketers insights for predicting consumer preferences and perceived durability.

Authenticity as fact or feeling: A dual‐process framework of consumer authenticity judgments
2026-05-25
R Kevin Smith, Katherine Du
AI
EN

This paper addresses the question of how consumers form authenticity judgments, rather than focusing on what authenticity is. Through a systematic review of authenticity research integrated with dual-process theories, the authors propose a conceptual framework positioning authenticity judgments as proceeding through two routes: a fact-based route involving deliberate analytical evaluation and a feeling-based route relying on intuitive processing and emotional resonance. Repentance‐driven fitness: Moral striving in a Tunisian context
2026-05-22
Hela Zouaoui, Russell Belk, Vitor Lima
AI
EN

This study examines how Islamic moral-spiritual meaning systems shape fitness consumption motivations and sustain ongoing moral striving among Muslim gymgoers. Using 13 months of ethnography and 17 interviews in a Tunisian gym, the researchers found that participants interpret exercise through Tawba (Islamic repentance and return), where diet lapses, missed sessions, and bodily discomfort become part of a recurring cycle of renewed intention and moral self-correction. How storytelling modality affects transportation and storyteller persuasion
2026-05-22

EN

This research investigates how communication modality (writing vs. speaking) affects storyteller self-persuasion, contrasting with prior audience-focused research. Through experimental studies, the authors find that storytellers are more transported and persuaded when writing rather than speaking their stories, which contradicts the pattern found for audiences. Discount now or later? The effect of payment framing on consumer preferences for discount timing in periodic payments
2026-05-13
Mijin Kwon, Song Oh Yoon
AI
EN

This research investigates how discount framing affects consumer preferences for the timing of discounts in subscription services. Through four main studies using online click-through behavior and experimental designs, the authors find that consumers prefer delayed discounts when the discount is expressed in the same unit as the recurring price (e.g., '$10, $10, $5'), because this identical framing creates a perception of a coherent improving sequence, leading to lower expectations of future prices. However, when discounts are framed differently (e.g., '50% off'), consumers revert to the standard preference for immediate discounts. This work contributes to pricing and temporal discounting literature by demonstrating that payment framing can reverse the typical preference for immediate benefits through sequence-continuation inferences.

Effectively communicating uncertainty: The persuasive impact of different types of hedges
2026-05-11
Demi Oba, Jonah Berger, Reihane Boghrati
AI
EN

This study examines how different types of hedging language (e.g., 'might,' 'probably,' 'I think') affect persuasion in consumer contexts. Using a multi-method approach combining experiments with machine learning analysis of millions of online reviews, the researchers found that hedges with personal perspective (vs. general) and those suggesting higher likelihood are more persuasive. The research contributes to marketing language literature by showing that hedging can protect communicators from backlash while maintaining persuasive impact, and that brands can leverage these effects through anthropomorphized agents.

This research investigates whether socioeconomic status (SES) influences consumer boycott participation and explores the underlying psychological mechanisms. The politics of impact: How political ideology shapes perceptions of the environmental impact of individual actions
2026-02-14
Aylin Cakanlar, Katherine White, Remi Trudel
AI
EN

This research investigates how political ideology shapes perceptions of the environmental impact of individual sustainable actions. Through seven studies, the authors find that conservatives perceive their sustainable behaviors as having less positive environmental impact than liberals do, which explains their lower engagement in such behaviors. From decision patience to process patience: A decision–process integration of the choice to wait and the experience of waiting
2026-01-28
Selin A. Malkoc
AI
EN

This commentary addresses the gap between two conceptualizations of consumer impatience: decision patience (choosing to wait for delayed rewards in intertemporal choice) and process patience (the emotional experience during waiting). Through theoretical integration, the author maps psychological drivers of intertemporal choice—including affective urges, cognitive representations, construal, subjective time perception, and future-self connectedness—onto Sweeny's Process Model of Patience. The key contribution is demonstrating where these two perspectives converge and diverge, proposing testable predictions about how choice patience and process patience can dissociate and how environmental cues may reduce uncertainty at the decision point while increasing impatience during the wait. This integration offers a more complete temporal model of impatience spanning from pre-decision through the entire waiting period.

EN

This paper investigates peer effects in user churn on a socially-connected digital platform and examines how firms can leverage network structures for customer relationship management. Using data from a massively-multiplayer online game, the authors develop a two-stage structural model that captures endogenous network formation and peer effects in churn, correcting for endogeneity bias through unobserved gamer heterogeneity. Key findings reveal that peer effects substantially amplify intervention effectiveness with an average social multiplier of 2.48, and that users vary more in their influence on peers' retention than in their own retention likelihood. The study contributes to CRM literature by demonstrating that a user's network value is primarily driven by network centrality measures rather than individual characteristics, offering actionable insights for targeted retention strategies.

本論文は、ソーシャル機能を持つデジタルプラットフォームにおけるユーザー離脱のピア効果を調査し、企業がネットワーク構造を顧客関係管理（CRM）に活用する方法を検討している。大規模マルチプレイヤーオンラインゲームのデータを用い、内生的なネットワーク形成とピア効果を捉える2段階構造モデルを構築し、観測されないゲーマーの異質性を通じて内生性バイアスを補正した。主要な発見として、ピア効果が介入効果を大幅に増幅させ（平均ソーシャル乗数2.48）、ユーザーは自身の継続可能性よりも他者の継続に与える影響において大きな差異があることが示された。本研究は、ユーザーのネットワーク価値が個人特性よりもネットワーク中心性指標によって主に決定されることを明らかにし、CRM分野に新たな知見を提供している。

EXPRESS: The Three-dimensional Effect in Logo Design: When Depth Cues Improve Brand Purchase Likelihood
2026-06-05
Taku Togawa, Naoto Onzo
EN

This research investigates how three-dimensional (3D) logo design affects consumer purchase likelihood compared to two-dimensional (2D) logos. Through multiple field, lab, and online studies, the authors find that 3D logos increase brand purchase likelihood because depth cues evoke mental representations of tangible forms, enhancing perceived logo realism and brand trust. However, these benefits are conditional: 3D logos are more effective in visually simple environments and for concrete offerings with high tangible dominance, while providing little advantage in complex environments or for abstract offerings. This study contributes to logo design literature by offering a nuanced theoretical framework for understanding when depth cues improve consumer responses and providing practical guidance for 3D logo adoption decisions.

EXPRESS: Modeling Dynamic Consumer Preferences from Few-shot Data: A Meta-Learning Approach
2026-06-03
Mingzhang Yin, Khaled Boughanmi, Anirban Mukherjee
AI
EN

This paper addresses the challenge of capturing and adapting to customer preferences when individual-level interaction data is limited, such as with new customers or short sessions. The authors propose Meta-Temporal Processes (MetaTP), a meta-learning framework that combines Transformer-based architecture to model sequential consumption patterns and enables personalization from few observations by transferring learned structure across customers. Empirical results on digital product consumption demonstrate that MetaTP outperforms benchmark methods in few-shot prediction tasks and reveals interpretable patterns of preference evolution. The contribution lies in providing firms with a scalable approach to optimize personalized recommendations during the 'lukewarm' customer journey stage, including product sequencing and session completion strategies.

本論文は、新規顧客や短期セッションなど個人レベルのデータが限られた状況で、顧客の嗜好を迅速に把握・適応するという課題に取り組んでいる。著者らは、Transformerベースのアーキテクチャを統合したメタ学習フレームワーク「Meta-Temporal Processes（MetaTP）」を提案し、少数の観測データから顧客間で共有構造を転移させることでスケーラブルなパーソナライゼーションを実現している。デジタル製品の逐次消費データを用いた実証分析では、MetaTPが少数ショット予測において既存手法を上回る性能を示し、嗜好変化の解釈可能なパターンを明らかにした。本研究の貢献は、顧客ジャーニーの「ぬるま湯段階」において、製品の推薦順序やセッション完了戦略を含むパーソナライズされたレコメンデーションの最適化手法を企業に提供した点にある。

EXPRESS: Haunted by a Bad Review: The Afterlife of Reading (Negative) Reviews in Consumer Experience
2026-05-26
Dena Yadin, Nira Munichor, Elanor F. Williams, Inbal Stockheim
EN

This research investigates whether product reviews affect not only purchase decisions but also the actual consumption experience itself. Across five studies using real consumer-generated reviews and product interactions, the authors found an asymmetrical assimilation effect: exposure to negative reviews before consumption leads to more negative product experiences, while positive reviews do not significantly enhance the experience. This effect occurs because negative reviews increase the salience of negative product attributes during consumption, though forewarning consumers or prompting independent opinion formation can mitigate it. The findings extend our understanding of review influence beyond the point of purchase and offer practical implications for marketers managing consumer experiences.

EXPRESS: Where Should Firms Implement Differential Privacy in Targeting? Implications for Profitability
2026-05-18
Gilian R. Ponte, Tom Boot, Thomas Reutterer, Jaap E. Wieringa
AI
EN

This study investigates where firms should implement differential privacy within their targeting workflow to balance privacy protection and profitability. Using a large-scale field experiment with 747,975 customers and simulations, the authors compare two novel targeting strategies: implementing differential privacy during model training versus at the targeting-decision stage. The key finding reveals that the decision-stage implementation yields approximately fivefold higher profits than the training-stage approach while maintaining strong privacy protection. EXPRESS: Ad Intensity Policies and Content Provision on Revenue-Sharing Content Platforms
2026-05-11
Yuansheng Wei, Lin Tian, Baojun Jiang
EN

This study investigates how online content platforms should design ad intensity policies to optimize creator incentives, content quality, and platform profitability. Using an analytical model with one platform and two competing content creators, the authors compare three policies: differentiated advertising (DA), uniform advertising (UA), and creator-set advertising (CA). Under symmetric creators and quality-independent marginal ad revenue, UA outperforms DA by intensifying quality competition, leading to higher content quality and platform profit, while CA weakens content investment incentives. EXPRESS: Picture This: How Review Valence Shapes the Quality and Helpfulness of User-Generated Photos
2026-05-04

EN

This paper investigates how review valence (positive vs. negative) influences the quality of user-generated photos in online reviews. EXPRESS: Separating the Artist from the Art: Social Media Boycotts, Platform Sanctions, and Music Consumption
2026-04-13
Daniel Winkler, Nils Wlömert, Jūra Liaukonytė
AI
EN

This paper examines how consumer demand for an artist's music changes when social media campaigns call for boycotts following misconduct. Using R. Kelly's case and an identification strategy that leverages variation in song-removal status and geographic demand, the authors distinguish between supply-side (platform visibility) and demand-side (intentional boycott) effects. EXPRESS: Does Puffery Sell? Evidence from Airbnb
2026-04-08
Michael Thomas
AI
EN

This study investigates whether puffery (positive but vague quality claims) influences consumer behavior on Airbnb. Using a hazard model that exploits within-listing changes to hosts' property descriptions, the research finds that adding a single puffed claim increases bookings by approximately 0.7 days per year, with minimal negative impact on ratings or review sentiment (less than 0.03 standard deviations). The findings challenge the legal assumption that consumers adequately discount puffed claims while supporting continued legal protection since puffery does not reduce consumer satisfaction. This research contributes to marketing and consumer protection literature by providing causal evidence on puffery's effectiveness in a real marketplace setting.

EXPRESS: Death or Muerte? Effects of Sales Language Use on the Consumption of Death-Related Products and Services
2026-03-27
Lexie Lan Huang, Xueni Li, Kimmy Wa Chan
EN

This research investigates whether using consumers' second language (vs. native language) in sales communications can increase consumption of death-related products and services (DRPS) such as life insurance and funeral services. Through eight studies including two field experiments and an eye-tracking study, the authors find that presenting DRPS messages in consumers' second language reduces fear of death by creating greater psychological distance, thereby increasing actual purchases. This study contributes novel insights to terror management theory and provides practical language-based strategies for marketers and policymakers to encourage proactive DRPS decision-making.

EXPRESS: Vocal Similarity, Timbre, and Persuasion in Consumer-Spokesperson Interactions
2026-03-25
Na Kyong Hyun, Michael Lowe, Aradhna Krishna
AI
EN

This study investigates whether similarity in vocal timbre between consumers and spokespersons influences persuasion. EXPRESS: Communication Patterns in Joint Decision-Making
2026-03-24
KELLEY GULLO WIGHT, HOLLY S. HOWE, DANIELLE J. BRICK, GAVAN J. FITZSIMONS
EN

This research investigates how people communicate during joint decision-making processes, addressing a gap in understanding dyadic communication patterns. Using nearly 200 shop-along observations, the authors identified four distinct communication patterns: coordination (inquiry and disclosure), contrast (persuasion and devil's advocate), build, and one-sided. Quantitative modeling revealed that decision partners navigate decisions non-linearly and that communication patterns affect immediate satisfaction outcomes. The study contributes a taxonomy integrating cross-disciplinary perspectives on dyadic communication to facilitate future interdisciplinary research.

Samsun Knight, Tsung-Yiou Hsieh, Yakov Bart
AI
EN

This study investigates the heterogeneity of TV advertising effectiveness across store characteristics and advertising levels using a large-scale panel of 135 US retail and restaurant brands. The authors find significant heterogeneity in TV advertising elasticity for over 93% of brands, yet firms' actual ad allocations follow simple heuristics rather than exploiting this heterogeneity—overinvesting in dense, high-income markets while underinvesting in areas with high concentrations of college-educated residents. The research demonstrates that identical-budget reallocations leveraging this heterogeneity could improve ad lift by a median of 2.35 percentage points and generate tens of millions in additional revenue, with 14-16% of brands with negative ROI potentially achieving positive ROI. This study contributes to advertising effectiveness literature by providing large-scale empirical evidence of suboptimal ad allocation practices and quantifying the substantial financial gains from heterogeneity-based reallocation strategies.

2026-03-04
Jake An, André Bonfrer, Christine Eckert
AI
EN

This study examines how the difficulty of goals selected during goal-enabling technology feature (GETF) adoption affects user engagement and retention in mobile apps. Using behavioral data from an investment app combined with hierarchical modeling, staggered synthetic control, instrumental variable regression, and a field experiment, the authors address self-selection and endogeneity concerns. EXPRESS: How Effective Is Suggested Pricing?: Experimental Evidence from an E-Commerce Platform
2026-03-01
Jessica Fong, Puneet Manchanda, Yu Song
EN

This study investigates how platform-suggested prices affect sellers' pricing decisions and sales outcomes on e-commerce platforms. Through a field experiment conducted with Mercari, a peer-to-peer marketplace, the authors manipulated whether sellers received suggested prices and the suggested price levels. Key findings reveal that a 30% change in suggested prices leads to a 5% change in listing prices, with stronger effects for new sellers and used items; lower suggested prices improve both sale probability and seller revenue. The research contributes to understanding how platforms can effectively guide seller behavior while preserving sellers' ability to utilize their private information.

AI

This research examines whether and how ingredient quantity information influences consumer food choices. This study examines whether and how firms can profit from consumer inattention, specifically left-digit bias in odometer readings, in the used car market. EXPRESS: Consumers Prefer that Corporations Donate Periodically
2026-01-29
Alexander Park, Yanyi Leng, Fausto J. Gonzalez, Jared Watson, Francesca Valsesia, et al.
EN

This research investigates how firms should communicate their corporate social responsibility (CSR) efforts, specifically whether periodic donations are more effective than equivalent lump-sum contributions. Through seven preregistered studies including two large field studies and five online experiments, the authors find that periodic donations (e.g., $20,000/month for 12 months) improve company outcomes such as reputation, customer engagement, and purchase likelihood compared to equivalent aggregate donations ($240,000/year). The benefits operate through two pathways: periodic donations signal consistent authentic prosocial motivation, which directly improves donor evaluations and also increases perceived donation impact. This research contributes to CSR communication strategy by highlighting consumers' sensitivity to consistency cues and the central role of perceived authentic motivation in granting charitable credit.

EXPRESS: How Social Cues Drive Popularity in NFT Art Markets
2026-06-05
Jaeyeon (Jae) Chung, Eric S. Park
EN

This study examines how consumers form preferences in NFT art markets where traditional quality standards are absent. Using a 150-day observational dataset of nearly 1.5 million observations, a pre-registered 20-wave panel study, and six experiments, the authors demonstrate that view counts serve as a key social cue, creating a 'snowballing' effect where artworks with early views accumulate disproportionately greater attention. The research contributes to theory on social influence by showing that social cues become the primary basis for preference formation when objective quality standards are unavailable.

EXPRESS: Personalization in Marketing Communication: A Meta-Analysis
2026-06-05
Martin Eisend, Dominika Niewiadomska, Guda van Noort
AI
EN

This meta-analysis examines the personalization paradox in marketing communication, where personalization may enhance persuasion through relevance but potentially trigger privacy concerns. Integrating 1,536 effect sizes from 290 studies across 229 papers, the research finds that personalization generally leads to persuasion success with negligible negative effects. Key findings reveal that personalization is most effective when data is tied to consumers' self-concept, and persuasion follows a J-shaped pattern with strongest effects at high personalization levels. This study provides empirical generalizations that resolve theoretical ambiguity and offer practical guidance for marketers on when personalization is most effective.

EN

This research investigates how power distance belief (PDB) influences consumer coupon usage behavior. EXPRESS: First-Party Content Production in a Competitive Media Market
2026-05-18
Xuelian Qin, Lin Tian, Bo Zhou
AI
EN

This paper investigates the optimal first-party (original) content production strategy for asymmetric competing video streaming distributors using an analytical model. The study reveals that existing content overlap and market price rigidity are critical factors affecting optimal strategies: high price rigidity combined with content overlap strengthens incentives for original content production, while low price rigidity with flexible pricing weakens these incentives. Importantly, when prices are optimally adjusted, first-party and third-party content shift from substitutes to complements, potentially creating a win-win-win outcome for distributors, third-party producers, and consumers. These findings challenge conventional assumptions about content competition and offer a strategic framework for managers to optimize content production investments based on resource asymmetry and market pricing dynamics.

EXPRESS: Product Portfolio Choices with Product Life Cycles
2026-05-18
Peichun Wang
AI
EN

This study examines how multi-product firms adjust their product portfolios in response to increased competition, focusing on the role of product life cycles (PLCs). Using data from the Chinese smartphone market, the author develops a structural model that incorporates firms' PLC expectations into product portfolio competition decisions. Key findings reveal that ignoring portfolio adjustments overstates consumer welfare gains from competition by 70%, and holding PLC expectations fixed understates firms' portfolio responses by 10% while overstating welfare gains by 9%. EXPRESS: Cushioning the Blow: Reducing Customer Attrition in Response to Price Increase Notifications
2026-05-11

AI
EN

This research investigates which type of price increase justification (cost, market, or quality) most effectively reduces customer attrition. EXPRESS: The Impact of Legal Protection of Trade Secrets on Advertising Spending: Insights from the Recognition of the Inevitable Disclosure Doctrine
2026-05-11
Sungkyun Moon, Jacqueline Chang, Kapil R. Tuli
EN

This study examines whether stronger legal protection of trade secrets influences firms' advertising spending decisions. Using the staggered recognition of the inevitable disclosure doctrine (IDD) across U.S. state courts as a quasi-natural experiment, the authors find that firms headquartered in states adopting IDD significantly increase their advertising spending. 2026-04-30

EN

This research investigates how internal linguistic consistency of political person brands on social media affects user engagement, using data from the 2020 U.S. presidential election with an empirics-first approach. EN

This research investigates whether AI-driven autonomous stores affect consumers' prosocial behavior compared to human-operated stores. EXPRESS: The ABCs of Marketing Principles: A Principles-Based Framework for Marketing Alignment and Adaptation
2026-04-13
Molly R. Burchett, Brian Murtha, Bernard J. Jaworski
AI
EN

This study examines how marketing principles function within organizations and how they can be effectively implemented to balance alignment and adaptation. Based on 38 in-depth interviews with marketing professionals across various firms and industries, the authors develop the 'ABC' framework comprising Articulating, Bridging, and Calibrating principles. The research reveals that less specific principles enable flexibility but risk misalignment costs (resource misallocation, mental burden, marketing sprawl), while more specific principles provide clarity but risk maladaptation costs (staleness, reduced professional development, opportunity costs). EXPRESS: Climate Communications in IPOs: Unpacking the Influence of Climate Disclosure Volume, Sender, and Message Characteristics
2026-04-13
Ankit Anand, Alok R. Saboo, Ritesh Adhyapak
AI
EN

This study examines how climate disclosure volume in IPO prospectuses affects firm performance, proposing and testing a U-shaped relationship grounded in damage-limitation logic. Using data from 1,586 IPO firms and BERT-based language models to analyze climate-related text, the authors find that low to moderate disclosure levels harm valuations by amplifying risk salience, while higher levels generate benefits through enhanced reputation and information transparency. EXPRESS: When Consumer Betrayal Spreads: Transmission and Compounding Effects in High-Stakes Situations
2026-03-31

EN

This research examines how consumers experience multiple betrayals from multiple actors in high-stakes situations, using the lived experiences of bereaved individuals during the COVID-19 pandemic. EXPRESS: Political Ideology Shapes Consumer Responses to Addictive Products

EN

This research investigates whether political ideology influences consumer responses to addictive products such as gambling, alcohol, and tobacco. 2026-03-27

This study examines how organizations can transform their new product development (NPD) processes to create inclusive products that address the needs of edge consumers—those who are underserved or unserved in the marketplace. EXPRESS: Not Just Bad Luck: Income-Based Gaps in Lottery Losses
2026-03-16

AI
EN

This study investigates whether lower-income consumers face additional disadvantages in lottery play beyond spending a larger share of their income. Using market-level and transaction-level data from California, the authors find that lower-income players are less responsive to high-jackpot games, use random number generators less frequently, and favor popular number combinations—behaviors that interact with pari-mutuel prize structures to reduce their expected earnings by approximately 7% per ticket, totaling nearly $3 million annually. The research evaluates alternative game formats including fixed non-jackpot prizes, mandatory random selection, and raffle-style jackpots, finding these interventions can reduce income-based disparities by up to 60%. EXPRESS: Dual Market Navigation
2026-03-12
Kellilynn M. Frias, Deidre Popovich, Ronald P. Hill
EN

This study examines how consumers navigate structurally distinct and institutionally separate markets simultaneously, extending cue utilization theory from the firm level to the market level. Using qualitative data from insured U.S. consumers who routinely shop for healthcare across the U.S.-Mexico border, the researchers develop the concept of 'strategic cue auditing'—a sensemaking process through which consumers evaluate and compare quality cues across different market contexts. The study identifies three cue auditing strategies (reactive, cultural-relational, and cross-comparative) and reveals how these strategies lead to 'cue misfires and misalignments' when consumers judge service quality across competing market logics. This research contributes by reconceptualizing cue utilization as structurally contingent and market-level, offering implications for understanding consumer behavior in cross-border, online-offline, and formal-informal market contexts.

EXPRESS: The Making-Up-For-Failure Nudge: Framing Subgoals as Opportunities for Redemption Increases Goal Persistence
2026-03-12
Shannon M. Duncan, Marissa Sharif
AI
EN

This research investigates how framing subgoals as opportunities to make up for past failures affects goal persistence. Through two longitudinal real-behavior studies, four lab studies, and one hypothetical study across domains including language learning, exercise, and word search games, the authors demonstrate that a 'making-up-for-failure nudge' increases goal-consistent behavior after setbacks. EXPRESS: Filling the Void: How Competing Brands Can Capitalize on a Brand Deletion
2026-03-01
Kristopher Keller, Harald J. van Heerde
AI
EN

This study investigates which competing brands benefit from brand deletions and how marketing mix adjustments influence post-deletion gains. Using a difference-in-differences analysis of 1,046 national brand deletions across 201 US markets over 10 years, the authors find that private labels benefit most from deletions, followed by sister brands, with rival national brands gaining the least, while overall category revenue does not fully recover. Journal of Consumer Research
Defining and Understanding Vintage
2026-06-15
Maren Hoff, Silvia Bellezza
AI
EN

This research addresses the question of what defines vintage products and how they differ from related concepts such as antique, secondhand, and retro items. Using a multimethod approach including interviews, surveys, experiments, and analysis of over 2,000 vintage products across 16 categories, the authors develop a unified framework identifying vintage as a composite formative construct determined by three dimensions: moderate age, iconicity, and scarcity. The key findings reveal that vintage products deliver personal benefits (heritage connection, authenticity), social benefits (coolness), and sustainability benefits (environmental responsibility), and that emphasizing these three dimensions enhances consumer responses more effectively for vintage than for new products. This research contributes a theoretical foundation for understanding vintage consumption and provides practical guidance for identifying and positioning vintage products in the marketplace.

Using a multimethod qualitative approach inspired by Reckwitz's sociology of singularities, the researchers examined a market for singularized digital goods. The study identifies four vectors of digital singularization, reveals variable ways technology mediates this process, and uncovers multiple tensions inherent in digital singularization. Key contributions include theorizing singularization as an inherently precarious, socio-technical process and advancing understanding of digital possession, value creation, and contemporary singularity markets.

Made With AI: Consumer Engagement with Social Media Containing AI Disclosures
2026-05-05
Stephan Carney, Ignacio Riveros, Stephanie Tully
AIartificial intelligence
EN

This research investigates how AI-generated content (AIGC) disclosures on social media affect consumer engagement. Using analysis of TikTok engagement data following their AIGC disclosure policy introduction and eight preregistered experiments, the authors find that AIGC disclosures reduce consumer engagement. Crucially, this reduction is not driven by concerns about content quality or AI aversion, but rather by diminished parasocial connection—the one-sided emotional bonds between consumers and creators—mediated by perceived creator effort. The study contributes a novel mechanism explaining consumer responses to AI disclosures and offers practical implications for platform policies and disclosure design, suggesting that signals of greater creator effort can mitigate engagement losses.

Inferences About Others’ Consumption Motives Influence What Consumers Expect from Similar Experiences
2026-05-01
Matthew J. Hall, Daniel M. Zane
EN

This research investigates how consumers' inferences about others' intrinsic or extrinsic motives for sharing experiences on social media influence viewers' expectations about those experiences. Through six studies, the authors demonstrate that when viewers infer a sharer had particular motives (intrinsic or extrinsic), they believe the experience is better suited to satisfy that same type of motive, which subsequently affects their own behavior during similar experiences. However, this effect disappears when viewers are highly familiar with the shared experience. The research contributes to understanding social influence on social media by showing that inferred motives not only affect social evaluations of sharers but also shape beliefs about the experiences themselves.

Brief Commentary: Theory Testing for Differences in Process – Hypothesizing, Testing, and Reporting Comparisons Between Indirect Effects
2026-04-16
Romain Cadario, Dan Schley, Gavan J. Fitzsimons
AI
EN

This paper addresses how consumer researchers should properly map theoretical hypotheses about process differences onto statistical tests when comparing indirect effects across contexts (moderated mediation) or mediators (parallel mediation). The authors demonstrate that different theoretical claims require different statistical approaches—for instance, claiming one conditional indirect effect is greater than another requires testing the index of moderated mediation, not simply reporting significance patterns. They provide two comprehensive tutorials covering theory-to-statistics mapping, appropriate model selection, and transparent reporting practices. The contribution lies in offering concrete guidance including pre-registration templates, data analysis procedures, and manuscript reporting examples to improve methodological rigor in process-focused consumer research.

A Framework for Understanding Consumer Response to the Depiction of Historically Underrepresented Identities in Marketing Communications
2026-04-14
Cory Haltman, Jianna Jin, Grant E Donnelly, Rebecca Walker Reczek
AI
EN

This research investigates why consumer responses to historically underrepresented identities (HUIs) in marketing communications vary, ranging from backlash to acceptance. Using moral foundations theory, the authors develop a framework proposing that while liberals respond positively to all HUI depictions due to their emphasis on Fairness, conservatives' responses depend on two dimensions: perceived agency and normativity regarding bodily purity. The findings reveal that conservatives react negatively only to HUIs perceived as high on both dimensions (e.g., obese, transgender, or hijab-wearing models), and this negative response is attenuated when the identity is low on either dimension or when HUIs appear as a numerical minority in campaigns. This framework contributes to understanding political ideology's role in advertising effectiveness and offers practical guidance for inclusive marketing strategies.

本研究は、マーケティング・コミュニケーションにおける歴史的に過小代表されてきたアイデンティティ（HUI）の描写に対する消費者反応が、なぜ反発から受容まで多様に分かれるのかを検討している。道徳基盤理論を用いて、リベラル派は公正性を重視するため全てのHUI描写に肯定的に反応する一方、保守派の反応は「主体性（agency）」と「身体的純潔に関する規範性（normativity）」という2次元の認知に依存するという枠組みを提案している。研究の結果、保守派は両次元が高いと認知されるHUI（肥満モデル、トランスジェンダーモデル、ヒジャブ着用モデル等）にのみ否定的に反応し、いずれかの次元が低い場合や、HUIがキャンペーン内で数的少数派として登場する場合には否定的反応が緩和されることが示された。本研究は、政治的イデオロギーが広告効果に与える影響の理解に貢献し、インクルーシブ・マーケティング戦略への実務的示唆を提供している。

Repair Service Signals: How Brand Repair Services Signal Unused Utility and Increase Product Repair
2026-04-13
Nathan Allred, Karen Page Winterich
AI
EN

This research investigates why brand repair services, compared to third-party services, are more effective at promoting product repair over replacement. Through two field studies and four lab experiments, the authors demonstrate that brand and brand-certified repair services signal unused utility in malfunctioning products, thereby increasing consumers' likelihood to repair rather than replace. The effect diminishes when consumers have domain expertise, when products hold sentimental value (as repair occurs without brand signals), or when product upgrades are salient (which attenuates the unused utility signal). This work contributes to signaling theory, unused utility literature, and offers practical implications for sustainability efforts and brand management strategies.

When Goods Were Odds: Do People Prefer Uncertain Goods After Uncertainty Is Resolved?
2026-04-08
Beidi Hu, Siyuan Yin, Alice Moon
AI
EN

This research investigates how people evaluate goods that originated from uncertain prospects (e.g., raffle prizes) after the uncertainty has been resolved. Through seven experiments with over 12,000 participants, the authors demonstrate an 'uncertainty spillover effect' whereby people prefer goods that came from uncertain origins compared to identical goods that were always known. The effect is explained by downward counterfactual thinking—uncertainty makes people perceive outcomes as superior relative to worse alternatives that could have occurred. This research contributes to consumer behavior literature by showing that uncertainty's influence persists beyond its resolution, affecting product evaluations and extending to associated products.

JA

本研究は、不確実な状況（例：抽選）から得られた商品が、不確実性が解消された後にどのように評価されるかを検討した。12,000人以上を対象とした7つの実験を通じて、「不確実性スピルオーバー効果」を実証し、不確実な起源を持つ商品は、最初から確定していた同一商品よりも好まれることを明らかにした。この効果は下方反事実思考によって説明され、不確実性により「起こりえたより悪い結果」と比較して現在の結果が優れていると知覚される。本研究は、不確実性の影響がその解消後も持続し、商品評価や関連商品にまで波及することを示し、消費者行動研究に貢献している。

2026-03-24

AI
EN

This research investigates whether company acknowledgment of customer participation in take-back programs can enhance sustainability perceptions and encourage repeat participation. Through seven studies including a field study, the authors demonstrate that simple acknowledgment increases customers' emotional attachment and sense of partnership with the company, leading to higher perceived sustainability and greater likelihood of repeat participation. Brief Commentary: A Framework for Detecting AI Agents in Online Research
2026-03-21
Felipe M. Affonso
AI
EN

This paper addresses the emerging challenge of detecting AI agents autonomously completing online surveys, which traditional attention checks fail to identify. The author introduces the Cognitive Trap Framework, which exploits architectural constraints of vision-language models to create questions that are difficult for AI but easy for humans. Testing against 1,007 human participants and 526 AI agents showed that cognitive traps detected 97.1% of agents while only flagging 4.1% of humans, compared to just 2.3% detection with traditional methods. The framework's key contribution is its adaptability—since AI model improvements are non-monotonic, new traps can be generated as agent architectures evolve, with validated tools made publicly available for researchers.

Limiting Accessibility: How Targeting Consumers with Disabilities Constrains Acceptable Prices for Innovations
2026-03-18
Musa Essa, Johannes Boegershausen, Gabriele Paolacci
AI
EN

This research investigates how mass-market consumers react to innovations targeting people with disabilities, particularly regarding price acceptability. Through nine main studies and six supplementary studies (twelve preregistered), the authors find that consumers view price premiums as less acceptable when products target people with disabilities compared to other segments, even when cost justifications are provided. The research identifies pity toward people with disabilities as the key psychological driver of this reaction, with variations in pity across different disabilities corresponding to differences in price premium acceptability. This work contributes by revealing a novel form of consumer paternalism that may paradoxically reduce marketplace inclusivity by penalizing companies that develop adaptive products for this underserved segment.

More Correlations Signal Causation: The Effect of Correlational Scope on Perceived Causality
2026-03-07
Yue Zhang, Gabriele Paolacci
AI
EN

This research investigates when consumers perceive statistical correlations as reflecting causal relationships in the context of big data and media reporting. Through eight preregistered studies, the authors demonstrate that a correlation (e.g., between tea drinking and bone health) is perceived as more causal when the plausible cause correlates with additional outcomes (correlational scope effect). This effect is attenuated when additional outcomes are weakly related to the focal outcome, mitigated under cause-last framing, and influences product choices. The findings contribute to understanding causal judgment by proposing category-based induction as an underlying mechanism and have implications for consumer well-being.

From Adaptation to Disruption: Structured Ambivalence as a Catalyst for Consumer-Led Institutional Work
2026-03-03
Anna E. Hartman, Eileen Fischer
EN

This study examines how consumers respond to ambivalence arising from conflicting institutional norms in the U.S. donor conception market. Using interviews, archival narratives, and case profiles, the authors develop a typology distinguishing between adaptation work (reconciling contradictions within existing arrangements) and disruption work (transforming institutional arrangements), each with individual and collaborative variants. The key finding reveals that adaptive work paradoxically reinforces the market system producing ambivalence, while structured ambivalence serves as a micro-level emotional catalyst driving the full spectrum of consumer-led institutional work. This research contributes to institutional theory by specifying the emotional and reflexive underpinnings of how consumers engage in institutional continuity and change within markets.

The Effect of Online Cart Composition on Cart Abandonment
2026-01-30
Liat Hadar, Yael Steinhart, Gil Appel, Yaniv Shani
AI
EN

This study investigates how the composition of online shopping carts affects cart abandonment rates. Using two large-scale field datasets and four controlled experiments, the authors demonstrate that carts with a higher proportion of hedonic (vs. utilitarian) products are more likely to be abandoned, an effect mediated by perceived hedonism and consumer guilt. The research contributes to e-commerce literature by identifying cart composition as a key factor in abandonment and offers a practical intervention: recommendation systems can reduce abandonment by suggesting utilitarian items to balance cart composition.

Do Not Think Ahead of Me: High‐Personalization Chatbots Can Backfire During the Goal Formation Stage
2026-06-15
Xingyu Chen, Minxue Huang, Xiaodong Xie
AIartificial intelligence
EN

This research investigates whether high-personalization AI chatbots are always beneficial for consumers across different decision stages. Through five studies, the authors find that high-personalization chatbots can backfire during the goal formation stage but not during the goal fulfillment stage, with reduced decision autonomy serving as the underlying mechanism. The study also reveals that consumers with interdependent selves (vs. independent selves) moderate this negative effect. These findings contribute to the chatbot personalization literature by highlighting the importance of considering specific decision stages when deploying AI chatbots.

Aging and Sustainable Consumption: An Investigation of the Role of Time Perspective
2026-06-15
Alessandro M. Peluso, Yeonjin Sung, Cesare Amatulli, Gianluigi Guido, Carolyn Yoon
AI
EN

This research examines how time perspective affects sustainable consumption among older consumers. Through five studies, the authors demonstrate that older consumers engage less in sustainable consumption due to adopting a shorter time perspective, but message cues prompting a longer time perspective can increase their environmentally friendly choices. The studies also reveal that perceived youthfulness moderates this relationship, with older consumers who feel younger being more likely to engage in sustainable consumption. These findings contribute theoretical insights to the aging and sustainability literature while offering practical implications for marketers targeting the growing older consumer segment.

When a Week Is Not A Week: How Temporal Boundaries Shift Regulatory Focus and Consumer Preference
2026-06-11
David DeFranza, Promothesh Chatterjee
EN

This study investigates whether consumer perception of sales events changes when they fall across calendar boundaries (e.g., next month) versus within the current period, even when objective time remains constant. Using analysis of over four million tweets spanning 11 years and two controlled experiments focused on Cyber Monday, the researchers found that crossing temporal borders increases subjective temporal distance, leading to more abstract construal and a promotion-focused regulatory orientation. This psychological shift results in consumers expressing stronger preferences for promotion-oriented product attributes over prevention-oriented ones when evaluating purchases across temporal boundaries. The findings contribute to marketing theory by introducing temporal borders as a non-linear factor that influences the relationship between temporal distance and consumer judgment, with practical implications for timing marketing communications.

本研究は、セールイベントが現在の期間内（例：今月中）ではなくカレンダー上の境界を越えた時点（例：来月）に位置する場合、客観的な時間距離が同じでも消費者の認知が変化するかを検証した。サイバーマンデーを対象に、11年間で400万件以上のツイート分析と2つの統制実験を実施した結果、時間的境界を越えると主観的な時間距離が増大し、より抽象的な解釈と促進焦点的な制御志向が生じることが明らかになった。この心理的変化により、消費者は時間的境界を越えた購買評価において、予防志向よりも促進志向の製品属性を有意に選好するようになる。本研究は、時間的境界が時間距離と消費者判断の関係に非線形的に影響する要因であることを示し、マーケティングコミュニケーションのタイミング最適化に実践的示唆を提供する。

Scarcity Coverage on Media and the Effects on Consumer Over‐Purchasing Behavior
2026-06-11
Loreta Axhami, Mirdaim Axhami, Agim Fjolla, Greta Siracusa
EN

This study investigates the relationship between media coverage of scarcity and consumer over-purchasing behavior, distinguishing between scarcity awareness from media exposure versus consumers' own active search. Using both experimental methods and longitudinal panel data from loyalty cards spanning 2019-2024, the researchers found that while exposure to scarcity increases purchase intentions, actual over-purchasing behavior (greater quantities and more frequent store visits) only occurs when both media-induced scarcity awareness and consumer-initiated search are simultaneously present. The study contributes to consumer behavior research by differentiating sources of scarcity salience and their effects, while offering practical implications for practitioners and policymakers regarding media's influence on stockpiling behavior.

More Than the Gift: How Giver Body Size Influences Recipient Perception
2026-06-11
Tongxi Wang, Sharla D. Biefeld, Abhi Bhattacharya
EN

This research investigates how a gift giver's body size influences recipients' appreciation for gifts. Across multiple studies, the authors find that larger body size increases gift appreciation through perceptions of warmth and effort, particularly for female givers, as recipients endorse stereotypes associating overweight individuals with warmth. The effect is attenuated when recipients hold strong weight-related prejudices or when gifts are framed as brand-provided. This work contributes to gift-giving literature by demonstrating that giver characteristics, beyond gift attributes, shape recipient responses and offers practical implications for marketers.

The Impact of Artificial Intelligence Co‐Experience on Consumer Loneliness: The Role of Culture and Experience Valence
2026-06-10
Yiming Qian, Carlos Velasco, Xiaoang Wan
AIartificial intelligence
EN

This research investigates whether AI-mediated co-experience can reduce consumer loneliness and improve service evaluations, and how cultural contexts moderate these effects. Through three studies employing both national-level cultural comparisons and individual-level cultural value measurements, the authors examined consumer responses to AI co-experience in positive and negative service contexts. Servant, Partner, or Competitor? How Embodied AI Role Positioning Influences Consumer's Purchase Intention Through Threat Perception and Feelings of Dominance
2026-06-05
Crystal T. Lee, Yung‐Cheng Shen
AIartificial intelligence
EN

This research investigates how different social role positioning of embodied AI (servant, partner, or competitor) influences consumer purchase intentions. Through four experimental studies grounded in social dominance theory, the authors demonstrate that servant AI generates the most favorable consumer responses via enhanced dominance perception and reduced threat, while competitor AI produces the least favorable outcomes. The studies further reveal that these effects are moderated by visual design morphology, humor modality, and individual mastery orientation, with curved designs and affiliative humor mitigating threat responses for competitor AI. This work contributes by establishing dominance signaling as a critical theoretical framework for understanding consumer-AI relationships and offering practical guidance for AI product positioning strategies.

Too Caring to Be Real? Autonomy‐Supportive AI, Intimacy Gains, and Authenticity Loss
2026-06-05
Thanh Tiep Le, Duy Khánh Phan, Bao Quoc Truong-Dinh
AIartificial intelligence
EN

This research investigates why autonomy-supportive and empathic AI communication simultaneously enhances perceived intimacy yet undermines perceived authenticity beyond a certain threshold. Using a staggered field rollout, longitudinal panel data, and a between-subjects experiment (N=207), the authors employ event-study Difference-in-Differences, System GMM, and experimental manipulation to estimate causal effects of Perceived Autonomy Support in AI Communication (PAS-AI). Results demonstrate that moderate PAS-AI increases relational intimacy through greater conversational elaboration and re-engagement, but overly consistent or fluent autonomy-supportive cues trigger authenticity decline, with users perceiving responses as scripted or 'too caring to be real.' The study contributes by reframing empathic AI as a matter of relational calibration rather than imitation, and shows that human-in-the-loop escalation and transparent AI disclosure can preserve relational trust.

New Money, Old Biases: Understanding Mental Accounting in the Cryptocurrency Era
2026-06-05
Filipa de Almeida, Tatiana K. Affonso, Ian J. Scott
EN

This study investigates how mental accounting biases apply to cryptocurrency contexts compared to traditional fiat currencies. Across four studies with 2,704 participants, the researchers examined consumer decision-making using both evaluative and behavioral purchase paradigms. The findings reveal that while classic mental accounting patterns generalize to cryptocurrency, consumers experience higher anticipatory guilt and lower purchase likelihood when spending cryptocurrency versus fiat currency. This study investigates whether Trustworthy AI certification labels can increase end-users' trust in and intention to use AI systems. How Artificial Intelligence Influences Consumer Reflection in Decision‐Making: The Role of AI Anthropomorphism and Reinforcement
2026-06-02
En Chen, Jie Meng, Safak Dogan
AIartificial intelligence
EN

This research examines how AI anthropomorphism and reinforcement influence consumer reflexivity in decision-making. Study 1 validated a self-reflexivity model showing that reflections on historical, social, and ideal selves sequentially drive self-evaluation, emotional responses, and behavioral intentions. Study 2, using a 2×2 experiment, found that while the core reflexivity pathway remains structurally stable across conditions, measurement invariance testing revealed a meaning-shift in the ideal-self construct rather than simple amplification effects. EN

This editorial examines the conceptual distinction between mental capacity and competence in consumer decision-making, addressing a growing marketplace concern as aging populations and mental illness intersect with complex consumer choices. The authors translate four clinical capacity criteria—understanding, appreciation, reasoning, and communication—into marketing-relevant contexts through conceptual analysis. 2026-05-30
Sarah Alhouti, Louvins Pierre, X.Z. Liu
AI
EN

This research examines how profile image realism and value transparency jointly influence consumer perceptions and trade behavior on C2C swapping platforms. Using three studies combining behavioral data analysis and experiments, the authors find that realistic profile images enhance perceived warmth and competence, thereby increasing trade intentions, particularly when value transparency is low. The Role of Intrinsic Quality in Reducing Consumer Xenocentrism: Evidence From a New World Wine Market
2026-05-28
Alvaro Luis Lamas Cassago, Mateus Manfrin Artêncio, Richard Paul Bagozzi, Janaína de Moura Engracia Giraldi, Fernando B. Da Costa
AI
EN

This study examined whether presenting intrinsic quality information about New World wines as superior to Old World wines can reduce consumer xenocentrism (C-XEN) and increase domestic wine purchase intentions. Using a representative consumer sample in an experimental setting, the researchers compared foreign versus domestic wines through expert and science-based quality information, with moderators including product involvement, trust in science, and material self. The findings revealed that superior quality information successfully reduced C-XEN, but this reduction did not translate into significantly higher purchase intentions for domestic wines, suggesting that self-enhancement and social-aggrandizement motives associated with foreign products remain resilient. The study advances C-XEN theory by demonstrating the potential and limitations of scientific quality communication in reducing preference bias, offering practical implications for domestic product marketing strategies.

More Than Saying “It's AI”: How Role Disclosure Transparency in AI‐Generated Ads Influences Persuasion
2026-05-28
Khanh Bao Quang Le, Hina Khan, Fangfang Li, Werner H. Kunz
AI
EN

This research investigates how AI role disclosure transparency—consumers' perception of how clearly an ad communicates AI's role in its creation—affects persuasion in AI-generated advertisements. Through four studies, the authors demonstrate that higher perceived transparency enhances ad creation process credibility, which leads to more favorable attitudes toward the ad. The positive effects of transparency are amplified when the disclosure motive is framed as reactive (rather than proactive) and when regulatory compliance signals are present. This research contributes to the understanding of strategic disclosure in AI-assisted advertising and provides a decision-making framework for marketers to optimize consumer responses to AI-generated content.

The Singular Effect of Expert Short Recommendation Lists
2026-05-28
Victor Mejía, Samy Guesmi
EN

This research investigates how recommendation list length (short vs. long) and source (human expert vs. aggregator) influence consumer perception, behavior, purchase intention, and satisfaction. Through four studies with 1,100 participants across different product categories, the authors find that short expert recommendation lists are perceived as more content-driven and diverse, leading consumers to spend more time evaluating items, select more expensive options, and avoid defaulting to the first recommendation. The study identifies the 'expert-shortlist effect' and demonstrates that mentalizing (understanding the recommendations) moderates purchase intention and satisfaction. These findings provide practical guidance for marketers and content creators in designing more effective recommendation lists.
